WHAT THE OLD WOMAN'S TERRACES KNEW

A mountain spring is failing in a dry year, and the village of Karrow has already decided whose fault it is. Illuminara has to find out what the survey measured, what it missed, and what an eighty-four-year-old woman who will not explain herself actually knows.

01 · The Cough

The spring at Karrow had made one sound for four hundred years, a steady pour into the stone trough in the square. In the last week of September it began to cough. Six hundred people heard it from their kitchens, and by the time Illuminara came up the switchback road on Tuesday at noon it was running at a little over a quarter of its autumn flow, and the village had decided whose fault that was.

"Maret Voss," said Tomas Reiner, who chaired the council and had not slept. He held a tin cup he was not drinking from. "Eighty-four years old, and she owns the whole slope above the spring. The water company offered her a price that would have put a borehole and a mains line in every house here. She said no. They asked to survey her terraces. She said no. We have eleven days of water in the tank." On Sunday evening the council would vote on a petition to buy the slope in the public interest, whatever she thought of it. A man beside him said the word miser, and Tomas did not correct him.

Edda Lind, the company's surveyor, had written to Illuminara herself, which no one on her side of the table had expected. She stood by the trough in a field jacket with a conductivity pen in her breast pocket and did not wait for introductions. "I wrote the hydrology report. There is no link between Voss's land and your spring. I put fluorescein into the fissure at the top stile and watched the spring for forty-eight hours. No dye. The water comes through the rock, the rock is draining in a dry year, and that is the whole of it." She said it flatly, and then she said, "I would stake my licence on that dye. I have also been uneasy for three weeks and cannot tell you why."

"Where did you put it?"

"In the fissure, on the boundary. She would not let me onto the terraces."

"Then you have measured how fast the rock answers," said Illuminara. She was looking up the slope, where long grey stairs of dry stone climbed into the cloud. "Nobody has measured how fast the walls do."

02 · The Slope

Maret Voss met her at the gate with a billhook in one hand, though she did not hold it like a threat. She was small and straight and wore three coats. She looked at Illuminara for as long as it took to decide something, and said nothing, as the village had warned she would.

"I would like to walk your terraces," Illuminara said. "I will not lift or measure anything on them without your say."

"Feet only," said Maret. "You look. You do not lift a stone."

There were forty-one walls. Illuminara counted them going up. It had not rained in twenty-three days and the fields between the walls were dust, but at the foot of the upper walls the stone was dark with damp, and the gaps left in the lowest course were packed with fresh brushwood. The walls leaned back into the hill. Behind them, in the way she felt the load in any built thing, like a held breath, she could tell there was a pillow of fist-sized stone a metre deep, set against the earth and recorded nowhere. Three walls near the top, seven, eight and nine, had slumped into their own fields. And the lowest six terraces had been scraped flat and footed in concrete, so that a tractor could turn. There was no damp at the foot of those at all.

"A digger," said Maret, when Illuminara stopped there. It was the first thing she had offered. "The council had a grant for improvement, in two thousand and four. I asked them to leave the lower six alone, and they were kind about it. The spring coughed that August. I told the water office, and in two thousand and six they sent a man with a very nice machine for two days. He said it was the weather. After that I stopped saying."

Illuminara did not argue with the grievance. She asked what the slope did when it rained.

"It drinks," said Maret. "Three nights. Then the walls wake at the foot, and after that the spring." It was in no survey and no one's notebook, and Illuminara wrote it down in front of her so that she could see it written. "I used to do a wall every autumn," Maret added, to the stones rather than to her. "These two years, half a wall. They fall faster than I mend."

03 · Three Causes

That evening in the school kitchen Illuminara wrote three headings on the flour board while Tomas, Edda and Karel Brandt, whose new orchard pump drew from the valley above the spring, watched with their coats on.

"You are going to say it is my pump," said Karel.

"I am going to say it might be, and then I am going to ask you to help me prove it is not." She wrote under each heading. If the rock was simply draining, nothing on the slope would answer rain for weeks, and the spring would stay at the conductivity Edda had found, five hundred and forty. If it was Karel's pump, the spring would sag in the hours he pumped, rain or not. If it was the walls, then after rain the foot of a standing wall would wake in about three nights, as Maret said, with water softer than the spring's, near three hundred and eighty, and a broken wall beside it, on the same slope, would not.

"That last one cannot be argued with," Edda said slowly. "The same rain, the same rock, one wall up and one wall down."

"Mrs Voss has three walls down. I would like one of them back up before the rain, and one left as it is." Illuminara turned to Tomas. "I need her to agree, and she will probably say no."

"Then what?"

"Then we measure what she allows." The forecast gave twenty-five to thirty millimetres on Thursday night, and nothing for three weeks after. Three nights from Thursday was Sunday afternoon, a little before the vote. "If she is right, you will have a result before you decide. If I am wrong about any one of these, you vote with nothing, and I will say so in the hall."

Karel took the pump log out of his pocket, where it seemed to have been all along, and laid it on the table. "Every hour since March," he said. "Read it."

04 · Wall Seven

Maret heard the proposal from her side of the gate, which was the only side she offered.

"You want a wall left down on purpose."

"For four nights. Then you rebuild it, with the troughs still in place, and we watch the second half."

"I do not leave a wall down on purpose." But she did not shut the gate. She asked what Illuminara meant to do with her hands, and Illuminara said she would carry a stone when asked and set none. Maret looked past her to the stile, where two village children had been pretending not to listen since morning. "Those two. Anja Reiner, and Teo, the baker's boy. They have been throwing stones at my crows since June, and they have good arms. They work for me. Not for the council."

Illuminara had planned the measuring troughs at the middle of each wall's foot. "At the left corner," said Maret, "by the stair. It wakes there first. You would have caught it a day late and called me a liar."

So the troughs went at the left corner: a lined slot under the lowest course, with a lip over a one-litre jug. Wall Seven went up over Wednesday and Thursday morning, eighteen metres of it, every stone set by Maret or by the two children, who learned within the hour not to hand her one she had not asked for. Illuminara lifted a single capstone at Maret's word and felt the pillow behind the wall take its weight and settle, like something breathing out. Wall Eight stood open to the sky beside it. Edda read both footings dry: four hundredths of a litre a minute at Seven, five at Eight. Maret watched the work on Seven for two days and did not look at Eight once.

It rained at ten on Thursday night, twenty-seven millimetres. Six hours later a brown surge of six-tenths of a litre a minute came out of the broken footing at Eight, muddy, at six hundred and ten, and by noon on Friday it was dry again. The water had run off. It had not stayed. At Seven the jug stayed empty through Friday and through Saturday, and on Saturday night Tomas telephoned the kitchen to ask whether she wished to say anything before morning. Edda, who had begun to hope, said that if the corner woke late the vote would go against Maret, and that there were ways to make a jug look hopeful. Illuminara said she had written three nights on the board and would read three nights or none, and went back to sit with the jug. Her own model had said two nights. Maret had said three.

On Sunday at five in the morning, fifty-five hours after the rain, the lip at the left corner of Wall Seven began to drip, and then to run. By four in the afternoon it gave seven-tenths of a litre a minute, clear, at three hundred and eighty-two.

Karel's log showed that his pump had taken about a tenth of the spring's missing flow and no more, and he did not enjoy being partly right. The dry year had taken some, said Edda, as she wrote the numbers out in her own hand, and she would not put a figure on it. But a dry year was exactly what the walls were for, and nine of them had stopped being able to do it.

05 · The Hall

On Sunday at six the school hall smelled of wet wool, and the motion to petition for the slope was on the table. Edda stood first, with two jugs and a trough lip still dripping, and told the village that her report was right about the rock and wrong about the slope, that her dye had asked the wrong question, and that she would sign the correction that night. Then she read the two columns. Nobody argued with the jugs.

Tomas did not look at the last row, where Maret Voss sat in three coats. He said that he withdrew the petition. His voice was not steady, and he did not yet say sorry.

The company's regional agent was on a laptop propped against the tea urn, and she was quick. Valmera would drop its offer for the slope. In its place it would pay for the rebuilding of the six scraped terraces, and for wages for two apprentices to learn the work, in return for its lower-valley draw being tied to the spring: if the flow fell below a litre and a half a second, the company would be the first to pump less.

"No money to me," said Maret from the back. Everyone turned. It was the longest thing she had said in the village in seventeen years. "No paper that says the walls are the company's, or the village's. They are mine until they are somebody's I choose. Pay the children. I will teach whoever comes, and I will say when a wall is done."

The agent said she would need that in writing. "You will have it in my hand," said Maret, "and it will be shorter than yours."

Illuminara said one thing. She asked for a post at the foot of Wall Three, the slow wall, with a lip and a jug and a painted mark, so that anyone in Karrow could see by the fourth night after rain whether the slope had drunk, without needing Maret's hip, or her name, or anybody's money. Maret considered it for some time. "That I will take," she said.

The village rationed itself to nine litres a head, and the tank stretched to eighteen days. On the twelfth night after the rain the cough in the square stopped, and what came after it was a thin steady run, and then a pour.

On Monday Wall Eight went up. Anja and Teo laid the first course wrong, and Maret turned a stone over with her boot and said, "Again," and they laid it again. A little before noon Tomas Reiner came up the slope with a stone from his own garden wall on his shoulder and set it without a word. Maret turned it with the toe of her boot. He had set it the wrong way round. "Again," she said, and he set it again.

By the end of October the spring ran at two and a half litres a second, a little under what it had been. The lower six terraces were still concrete and would take three autumns. Karel pumped at night. Two children gave up their weekends to a woman who corrected their stones with her boot. Maret Voss still owned every stone of her slope, and no one in Karrow called her a miser again within her hearing, which was not an apology, and was accepted as the beginning of one.

WHAT THE OLD WOMAN'S TERRACES KNEW

A mountain spring is failing in a dry year, and the village of Karrow has already decided whose fault it is. Illuminara has to find out what the survey measured, what it missed, and what an eighty-four-year-old woman who will not explain herself actually knows.

01 · The Cough

The spring at Karrow had made one sound for four hundred years, a steady pour into the stone trough in the square. In the last week of September it began to cough. Six hundred people heard it from their kitchens, and by the time Illuminara came up the switchback road on Tuesday at noon it was running at a little over a quarter of its autumn flow, three litres a second in a normal year, and the village had decided whose fault that was.

"Maret Voss," said Tomas Reiner, who chaired the council and had not slept. He held a tin cup he was not drinking from. "Eighty-four years old, and she owns the whole slope above the spring. The water company offered her a price that would have put a borehole and a mains line in every house here. She said no. They asked to survey her terraces. She said no. We have eleven days of water in the tank." On Sunday evening the council would vote on a petition to buy the slope in the public interest, whatever she thought of it. A man beside him said the word miser, and Tomas did not correct him.

Edda Lind, the company's surveyor, had written to Illuminara herself, which no one on her side of the table had expected. She stood by the trough in a field jacket with a conductivity pen in her breast pocket and did not wait for introductions. "I wrote the hydrology report. There is no link between Voss's land and your spring. I put fluorescein into the fissure at the top stile and watched the spring for forty-eight hours. No dye. The water comes through the rock, the rock is draining in a dry year, and that is the whole of it." She said it flatly, and then she said, "I would stake my licence on that dye. I have also been uneasy for three weeks and cannot tell you why."

"Where did you put it?"

"In the fissure, on the boundary. She would not let me onto the terraces."

"Then you have measured how fast the rock answers," said Illuminara. She was looking up the slope, where long grey stairs of dry stone climbed into the cloud. "Nobody has measured how fast the walls do."

02 · The Slope

Maret Voss met her at the gate with a billhook in one hand, though she did not hold it like a threat. She was small and straight and wore three coats. She looked at Illuminara for as long as it took to decide something, and said nothing, as the village had warned she would.

"I would like to walk your terraces," Illuminara said. "I will not lift or measure anything on them without your say."

"Feet only," said Maret. "You look. You do not lift a stone."

There were forty-one walls. Illuminara counted them going up. It had not rained in twenty-three days and the fields between the walls were dust, but at the foot of the upper walls the stone was dark with damp, and the gaps left in the lowest course were packed with fresh brushwood. The walls leaned back into the hill. Behind them, in the way she felt the load in any built thing, like a held breath, she could tell there was a pillow of fist-sized stone a metre deep, set against the earth and recorded nowhere. Three walls near the top, seven, eight and nine, had slumped into their own fields. And the lowest six terraces had been scraped flat and footed in concrete, so that a tractor could turn. There was no damp at the foot of those at all.

"A digger," said Maret, when Illuminara stopped there. It was the first thing she had offered. "The council had a grant for improvement, in two thousand and four. I asked them to leave the lower six alone, and they were kind about it. The spring coughed that August. I told the water office, and in two thousand and six they sent a man with a very nice machine for two days. He said it was the weather. After that I stopped saying."

Illuminara did not argue with the grievance. She asked what the slope did when it rained.

"It drinks," said Maret. "Three nights. Then the walls wake at the foot, and after that the spring." It was in no survey and no one's notebook, and Illuminara wrote it down in front of her so that she could see it written. "I used to do a wall every autumn," Maret added, to the stones rather than to her. "These two years, half a wall. They fall faster than I mend."

03 · Three Causes

That evening in the school kitchen Illuminara wrote three headings on the flour board while Tomas, Edda and Karel Brandt, whose new orchard pump drew from the valley above the spring, watched with their coats on.

"You are going to say it is my pump," said Karel.

"I am going to say it might be, and then I am going to ask you to help me prove it is not." She wrote under each heading. If the rock was simply draining, nothing deep would answer rain for weeks, though the damp at a wall's foot might seep shallowly all the same; what the spring was made of would not change, and it would stay at the conductivity Edda had found, five hundred and forty. If it was Karel's pump, the spring would sag in the hours he pumped, rain or not. If it was the walls, then after rain the foot of a standing wall would wake in about three nights, as Maret said, with water softer than the spring's, near three hundred and eighty, and a broken wall beside it, on the same slope, would not. And if that water reached the spring, the spring would go soft afterward, and not before.

"The pair of walls settles the walls," Edda said slowly. "The same rain, the same rock, one wall up and one wall down. It will not tell us what the spring does about it."

"No. The spring is the second reading." Illuminara nodded at the logger Edda had left in the trough in September. "If terrace water reaches it, it will soften after the walls wake. I will not be able to tell you how much of it that is. Only that it is some." She turned to Tomas. "Mrs Voss has three walls down. I would like one of them back up before the rain, and one left as it is. I need her to agree, and she will probably say no."

"Then what?"

"Then we measure what she allows." The forecast gave twenty-five to thirty millimetres on Thursday night, and nothing for three weeks after. Three nights from Thursday was Sunday afternoon, a little before the vote. "If she is right, you will know what the walls do before you decide, though not yet what the spring does with it. If I am wrong about any one of these, you vote with nothing, and I will say so in the hall."

Karel took the pump log out of his pocket, where it seemed to have been all along, and laid it on the table. "Every hour since March," he said. "Read it."

04 · Wall Seven

Maret heard the proposal from her side of the gate, which was the only side she offered.

"You want a wall left down on purpose."

"For four nights. Then you rebuild it, with the troughs still in place, and we watch the second half."

"I do not leave a wall down on purpose." But she did not shut the gate. She asked what Illuminara meant to do with her hands, and Illuminara said she would carry a stone when asked and set none. Maret looked past her to the stile, where two village children had been pretending not to listen since morning. "Those two. Anja Reiner, and Teo, the baker's boy. They have been throwing stones at my crows since June, and they have good arms. They work for me. Not for the council."

Illuminara had planned the measuring troughs at the middle of each wall's foot. "At the left corner," said Maret, "by the stair. It wakes there first. You would have caught it a day late and called me a liar."

So the troughs went at the left corner: a lined slot under the lowest course, with a lip over a one-litre jug. Wall Seven went up over Wednesday and Thursday morning, eighteen metres of it, every stone set by Maret or by the two children, who learned within the hour not to hand her one she had not asked for. Illuminara lifted a single capstone at Maret's word and felt the pillow behind the wall take its weight and settle, like something breathing out. Wall Eight stood open to the sky beside it. Edda read both footings dry on Thursday evening: nothing in either jug. Maret watched the work on Seven for two days and did not look at Eight once.

It rained at ten on Thursday night, twenty-seven millimetres. Six hours later a brown surge of six-tenths of a litre a minute came out of the broken footing at Eight, muddy, at six hundred and ten, and by noon on Friday it was dry again. The water had run off. It had not stayed. At Seven the jug stayed empty through Friday and through Saturday, and on Saturday night Tomas telephoned the kitchen to ask whether she wished to say anything before morning. Edda, who had begun to hope, said that if the corner woke late the vote would go against Maret, and that there were ways to make a jug look hopeful. Illuminara said she had written three nights on the board and would read three nights or none, and went back to sit with the jug. Her own model had said two nights. Maret had said three.

On Sunday at five in the morning, fifty-five hours after the rain, the lip at the left corner of Wall Seven began to drip, and then to run. By four in the afternoon it gave seven-tenths of a litre a minute, clear, at three hundred and eighty-two.

Edda's logger had sat in the trough since September, and on the nights Karel pumped it drew a small step down in the spring, about two-tenths of a litre a second, that closed when he stopped. Against the two and a quarter litres a second the spring was missing, that was about a tenth and no more, and he did not enjoy being partly right. The dry year had taken some, said Edda, as she wrote the numbers out in her own hand, and she would not put a figure on it. But a dry year was exactly what the walls were for, and nine of them had stopped being able to do it.

05 · The Hall

On Sunday at six the school hall smelled of wet wool, and the motion to petition for the slope was on the table. Edda stood first, with two jugs and a trough lip still dripping, and told the village what she could say and what she could not. Her report was right about the rock. Her dye had asked how fast the rock answered, and the slope had a second, slower way into the spring that a forty-eight-hour test could not see. How much of the spring came that way she could not yet say. She would sign the correction that night. Then she read the two columns. Nobody argued with the jugs.

Tomas did not look at the last row, where Maret Voss sat in three coats. He said that he withdrew the petition. His voice was not steady, and he did not yet say sorry.

The company's regional agent was on a laptop propped against the tea urn, and she was quick. Valmera drew from the lower valley under a licence written against the spring's flow, she said; a spring that failed took the licence with it, and a taking petition resting on a survey its own surveyor had just corrected would not survive review. Valmera would drop its offer for the slope. In its place it would pay for the rebuilding of the six scraped terraces, and for wages for two apprentices to learn the work, and its lower-valley draw would be tied to the spring: if the flow fell below a litre and a half a second, as read at the village trough, the company would be the first to pump less.

"No money to me," said Maret from the back. Everyone turned. It was the longest thing she had said in the village in seventeen years. "No paper that says the walls are the company's, or the village's. They are mine until they are somebody's I choose. Pay the children. I will teach whoever comes, and I will say when a wall is done."

The agent said she would need that in writing. "You will have it in my hand," said Maret, "and it will be shorter than yours." The agent typed for some time, and then turned the screen toward the room. Valmera accepted: no title, no water rights, the terraces under Maret's say, and its signature beneath hers and the council's.

Illuminara said one thing. She asked for a post at the foot of Wall Three, the slow wall, with a lip and a jug and a painted mark, so that anyone in Karrow could see by the fourth night after rain whether the slope had drunk, nine days before the trough could show it, without needing Maret's hip, or her name, or anybody's money. The council clerk could read it, and a dry jug after a good rain would put the company's promise on the table before the spring fell to the line. Maret considered it for some time. "That I will take," she said.

On Monday, the morning after the vote, Wall Eight went up. Anja and Teo laid the first course wrong, and Maret turned a stone over with her boot and said, "Again," and they laid it again. Illuminara set the trough at Eight's left corner, as before. The forecast still held no rain for three weeks, so the second half of the trial, Eight standing under the same sky as Seven, would have to wait for the next one, and she told Maret so. "I have waited longer for less," said Maret. A little before noon Tomas Reiner came up the slope with a stone from his own garden wall on his shoulder and set it without a word. Maret turned it with the toe of her boot. He had set it the wrong way round. "Again," she said, and he set it again.

The village rationed itself to nine litres a head, and the tank stretched to eighteen days. On the twelfth night after the rain the cough in the square stopped, and what came after it was a thin steady run, and then a pour. Edda's logger read the returning water at four hundred and sixty-eight, softer than the rock's five hundred and forty and harder than the water at Maret's left corner. On a plain mixture that made a little under half of it terrace water, and she would not call it better than that: rain had fallen on the rock as well, and she could not say how much of the rest it had refilled. The bedrock had not been ruled out. It had been ruled down, from the whole of the spring to a share of it.

By the end of October the spring ran at two and a half litres a second, a little under what it had been. The lower six terraces were still concrete and would take three autumns. Karel pumped at night. Two children gave up their weekends to a woman who corrected their stones with her boot. Maret Voss still owned every stone of her slope, and no one in Karrow called her a miser again within her hearing, which was not an apology, and was accepted as the beginning of one.

KAJ SO VEDELE STARKINE TERASE

01 · Kašelj

Izvir v Karrowu je štiristo let oddajal en sam zvok, enakomeren tok v kamnito korito na trgu. Zadnji teden septembra je začel kašljati. Šeststo ljudi ga je slišalo iz svojih kuhinj in do torka opoldne, ko je Illuminara po serpentinasti cesti prišla v vas, je tekel z malo več kot četrtino običajnega jesenskega pretoka, ki je v navadnem letu znašal tri litre na sekundo, vas pa se je že odločila, kdo je kriv.

"Maret Voss," je rekel Tomas Reiner, predsedujoči svetu, ki ni spal. V roki je držal pločevinasto skodelico, iz katere ni pil. "Štiriinosemdeset let ima in vse pobočje nad izvirom je njeno. Vodno podjetje ji je ponudilo ceno, s katero bi lahko izvrtali vrtino in napeljali vodovod v vsako tukajšnjo hišo. Rekla je ne. Prosili so jo, da bi izmerili njene terase. Rekla je ne. V rezervoarju imamo vode za enajst dni." V nedeljo zvečer bo svet glasoval o predlogu za odkup pobočja v javnem interesu, pa naj si ona misli, kar hoče. Moški ob njem je izrekel besedo skopuhinja in Tomas ga ni popravil.

Edda Lind, geodetka podjetja, je Illuminari pisala sama, česar ni pričakoval nihče na njeni strani mize. Ob koritu je stala v terenski jakni, v prsnem žepu pa je imela merilnik prevodnosti, in ni čakala na predstavitve. "Jaz sem napisala hidrološko poročilo. Med Vossovim zemljiščem in vašim izvirom ni povezave. V razpoko pri zgornjem prehodu sem vlila fluorescein in oseminštirideset ur opazovala izvir. Nobenega barvila. Voda prihaja skozi skalo, skala se v sušnem letu prazni in to je vse." Izrekla je brez ovinkarjenja, nato pa dodala: "Za tisto barvilo bi zastavila svojo licenco. Že tri tedne mi je tudi nelagodno, pa vam ne znam povedati zakaj."

"Kam ste ga vlili?"

"V razpoko, na meji. Na terase me ni spustila."

"Potem ste izmerili, kako hitro odgovori skala," je rekla Illuminara. Gledala je po pobočju navzgor, kjer so dolge sive stopnice suhozidov plezale v oblak. "Nihče pa ni izmeril, kako hitro odgovorijo zidovi."

02 · Pobočje

Maret Voss jo je pričakala pri vratih s kosirjem v eni roki, čeprav ga ni držala kot grožnjo. Bila je majhna in vzravnana ter oblečena v tri plašče. Illuminaro je gledala toliko časa, kolikor ga je potrebovala, da se je o nečem odločila, nato pa ni rekla ničesar, prav kakor so jo opozorili vaščani.

"Rada bi prehodila vaše terase," je rekla Illuminara. "Brez vašega dovoljenja na njih ne bom ničesar dvignila ali izmerila."

"Samo noge," je rekla Maret. "Gledate. Kamna ne dvignete."

Zidov je bilo enainštirideset. Illuminara jih je med vzponom štela. Deževalo ni triindvajset dni in polja med zidovi so bila prašna, vendar je bil kamen ob vznožju zgornjih zidov temen od vlage, vrzeli v najnižji vrsti pa so bile natlačene s svežim dračjem. Zidovi so se naslanjali nazaj v hrib. Za njimi je na način, na katerega je čutila obremenitev v vsaki grajeni stvari, kakor zadržan dih, zaznala meter globoko blazino iz kamenja v velikosti pesti, položeno ob zemljo in nikjer zabeleženo. Trije zidovi pri vrhu, sedmi, osmi in deveti, so se sesedli na svoja polja. Najnižjih šest teras pa je bilo zravnanih in temeljenih z betonom, da se je lahko traktor obrnil. Ob vznožju teh ni bilo prav nič vlage.

"Bager," je rekla Maret, ko se je Illuminara tam ustavila. To je bilo prvo, kar je ponudila sama. "Svet je imel denar za izboljšave, leta dva tisoč štiri. Prosila sem jih, naj spodnjih šest pustijo pri miru, in bili so zelo prijazni glede tega. Izvir je tistega avgusta kašljal. Povedala sem vodnemu uradu in leta dva tisoč šest so za dva dni poslali moža z zelo lepim strojem. Rekel je, da je krivo vreme. Potem sem nehala govoriti."

Illuminara se ni prerekala z zamero. Vprašala je, kaj pobočje naredi, ko dežuje.

"Pije," je rekla Maret. "Tri noči. Potem se zidovi prebudijo ob vznožju in za njimi izvir." Tega ni bilo v nobeni meritvi in v nobenem zvezku, Illuminara pa je zapisala pred njo, da je Maret lahko videla svoje besede na papirju. "Včasih sem vsako jesen naredila en zid," je Maret dodala, bolj kamnom kakor njej. "Ti dve leti pol zidu. Padajo hitreje, kakor jih popravljam."

03 · Trije vzroki

Tisti večer je Illuminara v šolski kuhinji na pomokano tablo napisala tri naslove, medtem ko so Tomas, Edda in Karel Brandt, čigar nova črpalka v sadovnjaku je črpala iz doline nad izvirom, v plaščih opazovali.

"Rekli boste, da je kriva moja črpalka," je rekel Karel.

"Rekla bom, da je lahko, potem pa vas bom prosila, da mi pomagate dokazati, da ni." Pod vsak naslov je nekaj zapisala. Če se je skala preprosto praznila, se globoko pod zemljo na dež več tednov ne bi odzvalo nič, čeprav bi plitva voda lahko vseeno pronicala ob vznožju zidu; sestava izvira se ne bi spremenila in ostala bi pri prevodnosti petsto štirideset, ki jo je izmerila Edda. Če je kriva Karelova črpalka, bi izvir upadal v urah, ko je črpal, z dežjem ali brez njega. Če so bili krivi zidovi, bi se po dežju vznožje stoječega zidu prebudilo po približno treh nočeh, kakor je rekla Maret, z vodo, mehkejšo od izvirske, blizu tristo osemdeset, podrt zid ob njem na istem pobočju pa ne. In če bi ta voda dosegla izvir, bi se izvir zmehčal zatem, ne prej.

"Par zidov pojasni zidove," je počasi rekla Edda. "Isti dež, ista skala, en zid stoji, drugi je podrt. Ne bo pa nam povedal, kaj ima izvir s tem."

"Ne. Izvir je druga meritev." Illuminara je pokimala proti beležilniku, ki ga je Edda septembra pustila v koritu. "Če voda s teras pride do njega, se bo po tem, ko se zidovi prebudijo, zmehčal. Ne bom vam mogla povedati, kolikšen del vode je to. Samo, da ga je nekaj." Obrnila se je k Tomasu. "Gospa Voss ima tri podrte zidove. Enega bi rada postavila nazaj pred dežjem, drugega pa pustila takega, kot je. Potrebujem njeno soglasje in verjetno bo rekla ne."

"In potem?"

"Potem izmerimo, kar dovoli." Napoved je za četrtkovo noč obetala petindvajset do trideset milimetrov, nato pa tri tedne nič. Tri noči od četrtka so pomenile nedeljsko popoldne, malo pred glasovanjem. "Če ima prav, boste pred odločitvijo vedeli, kaj počnejo zidovi, čeprav še ne, kaj z njihovo vodo naredi izvir. Če se motim o čemerkoli od tega, boste glasovali brez dokazov in jaz bom to povedala v dvorani."

Karel je iz žepa potegnil dnevnik črpanja, kakor da bi bil ves čas tam, in ga položil na mizo. "Vsaka ura od marca," je rekel. "Preberite ga."

04 · Sedmi zid

Maret je predlog poslušala s svoje strani vrat, edine strani, ki jo je ponudila.

"Hočete, da zid namenoma pustim podrt."

"Za štiri noči. Potem ga obnovite, korita pa ostanejo na mestu, in opazujemo drugo polovico."

"Zidu namenoma ne puščam podrtega." Vendar vrat ni zaprla. Vprašala je, kaj namerava Illuminara početi z rokami, in Illuminara je rekla, da bo nesla kamen, ko jo bodo prosili, sama pa ne bo položila nobenega. Maret je pogledala mimo nje proti prehodu, kjer sta se vaška otroka že od jutra pretvarjala, da ne prisluškujeta. "Tista dva. Anja Reiner in Teo, pekov fant. Od junija mečeta kamenje v moje vrane in imata dobri roki. Delata zame. Ne za svet."

Illuminara je merilni koriti načrtovala na sredini vznožja vsakega zidu. "Pri levem vogalu," je rekla Maret, "ob stopnici. Tam se najprej prebudi. Ujeli bi ga dan prepozno in me razglasili za lažnivko."

Koriti sta torej šli k levemu vogalu: obložena reža pod najnižjo vrsto z robom nad litrskim vrčem. Sedmi zid so postavili v sredo in četrtek zjutraj, vseh osemnajst metrov, vsak kamen pa je položila Maret ali eden od otrok, ki sta se v eni uri naučila, naj ji ne podajata tistega, za katerega ni prosila. Illuminara je na Maretino besedo dvignila en sam vrhnji kamen in čutila, kako je blazina za zidom prevzela njegovo težo in se polegla, kakor da bi nekaj izdihnilo. Osmi zid je ob njem ostal odprt proti nebu. Edda je v četrtek zvečer obe vznožji izmerila suhi: v nobenem vrču ni bilo ničesar. Maret je dva dni opazovala delo na Sedmem in niti enkrat ni pogledala Osmega.

V četrtek ob desetih zvečer je padlo sedemindvajset milimetrov dežja. Šest ur pozneje je iz podrtega vznožja Osmega brizgnil rjav tok šestih desetink litra na minuto, blaten, s prevodnostjo šeststo deset, do petkovega poldneva pa je bil spet suh. Voda je odtekla. Ni ostala. Pri Sedmem je vrč ostal prazen ves petek in vso soboto, v soboto zvečer pa je Tomas poklical v kuhinjo in vprašal, ali želi pred jutrom kaj povedati. Edda, ki je začela upati, je rekla, da bo glasovanje šlo proti Maret, če se bo vogal prebudil prepozno, in da obstajajo načini, kako narediti vrč videti obetaven. Illuminara je rekla, da je na tablo napisala tri noči in bo prebrala tri noči ali nič, nato pa se je vrnila čakat ob vrču. Njen lastni model je napovedal dve noči. Maret je rekla tri.

V nedeljo ob petih zjutraj, petinpetdeset ur po dežju, je rob pri levem vogalu Sedmega zidu začel kapljati in nato teči. Do štirih popoldne je dajal sedem desetink litra na minuto, čistega, pri tristo dvainosemdeset.

Eddin beležilnik je bil v koritu od septembra in v nočeh, ko je Karel črpal, je v pretoku izvira zabeležil majhen padec, približno dve desetinki litra na sekundo, ki je izginil, ko je nehal. V primerjavi z dvema litroma in četrt na sekundo, kolikor jih je izviru manjkalo, je bila to približno desetina in nič več, česar ni z veseljem priznal. Sušno leto je vzelo nekaj, je rekla Edda, ko je številke lastnoročno zapisovala, in temu ni hotela določiti deleža. Toda zidovi so obstajali prav zaradi sušnega leta, devet od njih pa tega ni več zmoglo.

05 · Dvorana

V nedeljo ob šestih je šolska dvorana dišala po mokri volni, predlog za zahtevo po pobočju pa je bil na mizi. Edda je vstala prva z dvema vrčema in robom korita, ki je še kapljal, ter vaščanom povedala, kaj lahko reče in česa ne. Njeno poročilo je imelo prav o skali. Njeno barvilo je vprašalo, kako hitro odgovori skala, pobočje pa je imelo drugo, počasnejšo pot v izvir, ki je oseminštirideseturni preizkus ni mogel videti. Kolikšen del izvira prihaja po tej poti, še ni mogla povedati. Popravek bo podpisala še tisto noč. Nato je prebrala oba stolpca. Z vrčema se ni nihče prepiral.

Tomas ni pogledal zadnje vrste, kjer je Maret Voss sedela v treh plaščih. Rekel je, da umika predlog. Glas se mu ni umiril in še vedno ni rekel oprostite.

Regionalna zastopnica podjetja se je oglasila s prenosnika, naslonjenega ob čajni kotel, in bila je hitra. Valmera je črpala iz spodnje doline po dovoljenju, vezanem na pretok izvira, je rekla; izvir, ki bi presahnil, bi s seboj odnesel tudi dovoljenje, predlog za odvzem zemljišča, oprt na meritev, ki jo je lastna geodetka pravkar popravila, pa ne bi prestal revizije. Valmera bo umaknila ponudbo za pobočje. Namesto tega bo plačala obnovo šestih zravnanih teras in plače za dva vajenca, ki se bosta naučila dela, njen odjem v spodnji dolini pa bo vezan na izvir: če pretok, odčitan pri vaškem koritu, pade pod liter in pol na sekundo, bo podjetje prvo, ki bo črpalo manj.

"Meni nič denarja," je rekla Maret iz zadnje vrste. Vsi so se obrnili. To je bilo najdaljše, kar je v vasi povedala v sedemnajstih letih. "Nobenega papirja, na katerem piše, da so zidovi last podjetja ali vasi. Moji so, dokler ne postanejo last nekoga, ki ga izberem jaz. Plačajte otrokoma. Učila bom vsakogar, ki pride, in jaz bom povedala, kdaj je zid končan."

Zastopnica je rekla, da bo to potrebovala pisno. "Dobili boste v moji pisavi," je rekla Maret, "in krajše bo od vašega." Zastopnica je nekaj časa tipkala in nato zaslon obrnila proti dvorani. Valmera je sprejela: nobenega lastništva, nobenih vodnih pravic, terase pod Maretino besedo ter podpis podjetja pod njenim in podpisom sveta.

Illuminara je rekla eno stvar. Prosila je za drog ob vznožju Tretjega zidu, počasnega zidu, z robom, vrčem in pobarvano oznako, da bi lahko vsakdo v Karrowu četrto noč po dežju videl, ali je pobočje pilo, devet dni preden bi to lahko pokazalo korito, ne da bi potreboval Maretin kolk ali njeno ime ali denar kogarkoli. Prebral bi ga lahko uradnik sveta in suh vrč po dobrem dežju bi obljubo podjetja postavil na mizo, še preden bi izvir padel do črte. Maret je o tem dolgo razmišljala. "To sprejmem," je rekla.

V ponedeljek, jutro po glasovanju, so postavili Osmi zid. Anja in Teo sta prvo vrsto položila narobe, Maret pa je kamen z nogo obrnila in rekla: "Še enkrat," in položila sta ga znova. Illuminara je korito postavila pri levem vogalu Osmega, kakor prej. Napoved je še vedno kazala tri tedne brez dežja, zato bo morala druga polovica preizkusa, Osmi pod istim nebom kakor Sedmi, počakati na naslednjega, in to je povedala Maret. "Na manj sem čakala dlje," je rekla Maret. Malo pred poldnevom je Tomas Reiner prišel po pobočju s kamnom iz zidu svojega vrta na rami in ga brez besed položil. Maret ga je obrnila s konico škornja. Položil ga je narobe. "Še enkrat," je rekla in položil ga je znova.

Vas je porabo omejila na devet litrov na osebo in zaloga v rezervoarju je zdržala osemnajst dni. Dvanajsto noč po dežju je kašelj na trgu prenehal in za njim je prišel tanek enakomeren tok, nato pa izliv. Eddin beležilnik je vračajoči se vodi izmeril štiristo oseminšestdeset, mehkeje od skale pri petsto štirideset in trše od vode pri Maretinem levem vogalu. Po preprostem mešanju je to pomenilo, da je je malo manj kot polovica terasne vode, vendar Edda tega ni hotela označiti za nič več kakor približek: dež je padel tudi na skalo in ni mogla povedati, kolikšen del preostanka je napolnil. Kamninska podlaga ni bila izključena. Zmanjšali so ji pomen, od celotnega izvira na njegov delež.

Do konca oktobra je izvir tekel z dvema litroma in pol na sekundo, malo manj kakor prej. Spodnjih šest teras je bilo še vedno betonskih in potrebovale bodo tri jeseni. Karel je črpal ponoči. Dva otroka sta svoje konce tedna odstopila ženski, ki je njune kamne popravljala s škornjem. Maret Voss je še vedno imela v lasti vsak kamen svojega pobočja in nihče v Karrowu je v njenem sluhu ni več imenoval skopuhinja, kar ni bilo opravičilo, vendar je bilo sprejeto kot njegov začetek.

THE DAY THAT KEPT COMING BACK

In the river town of Ostrel one Tuesday keeps restarting at its edges, and the thing that is replaying it is not an enemy but a visitor who cannot leave its worst hour. Illuminara has to read the pattern, and a father has to decide what he will and will not be given.

01 · The Market Clock

On the ninth Tuesday the market clock in Ostrel struck six, and the stallholders on the riverside side of the square began to unpack the same crates they had unpacked an hour before. Nobody on the hill side noticed at first. The hill side was moving toward Wednesday as it always had, a little late, a little thin, like a coat with the lining worn through.

Illuminara came down the Varna road at noon by the hill's clock and found a town of three thousand one hundred people standing on two different days. At the ferry landing, the school and the market, it was early morning again. In the streets between, it was afternoon, and in the streets beyond that the afternoons had started to stutter: a baker whose oven went cold between one loaf and the next, a woman who walked into a kitchen and could not say which day she had left it.

Councillor Mira Halse met her at the toll bridge with a map on which the edge districts were inked in red. "We have a visitor," she said. "The Guest of the Ninth Bridge. It has stood there since the first Tuesday and says it is sorry. The council meets at four. I mean to put a motion to end this."

"End it how?"

"By vote. By whatever vote it takes." Halse folded the map. "Every day it comes back, the red grows by a street. We don't have the Tuesdays to spare."

Illuminara asked to see the Guest first, and she asked one other thing. She had Wren Odira stay on the hill side with a stopwatch and a ledger, because someone had to keep ordinary time while she stood in the loop's. A watch that has never been inside an hour cannot be talked out of what it says.

02 · Three Replays

The Guest stood at the rail of the Ninth Bridge landing, tall as a ferry mast and dressed in a ferryman's coat that did not quite agree with its edges. It saw her at once, and all at once: it said that she had arrived, was arriving, and had left by the toll road, and that it hoped none of these was a discourtesy. It perceived the whole of time the way a person perceives a room. It could not walk out of the one hour in which it had failed.

The hour ran from ten past four to ten past five. In it, the Guest had been carrying something, and it had not delivered it. Each Tuesday at ten past five the edge districts snapped back to six in the morning, and the Guest began again at ten past four with the same weight in its hands, and the same shame.

Illuminara took three replays to read it. She did not argue with the Guest or comfort it. She carried a pocket watch and a notebook that she had brought down the road from Wednesday, and she kept them inside her coat, where the hour had no claim on them. At each snap the town forgot and she did not. By the third she had a page of marks.

Two things never changed. At nine minutes and thirty seconds past four, Anselm Rook came down the infirmary stair beside the ferry office and stood at the landing rail, because his daughter Pia was upstairs in the infirmary bed, nine years old, with a heart that would stop that evening, and he could not breathe in the room. And at eleven minutes and twenty seconds past four the ferry horn blew its warning and covered the Guest's first sentence, which began, I have been carrying.

Two things changed every time. The Guest lit a ferry lantern, and its flame was white on her first replay, amber on her second, green on her third. (It went round a fixed rotation of four, she would learn, and she had arrived at the start of a round.) And the Guest offered it to a different person. First to Anselm, who looked at it as a man looks at a lit window in a house he will not enter, and climbed the stair. Then to the ferrymaster, Cato Brandt, who put his hands behind his back. Then to Pia's teacher, Joss Maren, who took it as far as the infirmary door, where Anselm shut the door on both of them.

"It tries someone new each time," Illuminara told Wren afterward, by the hill's clock. "That is what a message looks like when it cannot find the person it was meant for."

03 · The Man Who Stayed Upstairs

"It could be me," Anselm Rook said. He had come out to the toll bridge at dusk, and he spoke to the river rather than to her. "Isn't that what you're thinking? That I am making it. That the lamp is only my grief, standing in a coat."

"It is the best rival I have," said Illuminara. "A lantern made of your grief would follow you. When you were present it would show you; if you left, it would change or go out. I have a prediction, and it can embarrass me. On the fourth replay the flame will be blue, whether or not you come down the stair."

He did not answer for a while. "You want me to stay upstairs."

"I want to know whether you will. It is your hour with her, and you may spend it as you like. I will not ask twice."

Halse, who had come out behind him with the council's motion in her hand, said what she had been waiting to say. If Illuminara could end the loop only with Rook's cooperation, then the motion should simply bind him. A town could vote to take a man's hour. Illuminara said that a town could vote to do many things, and that none of them would put Pia Rook back in the afternoon or get the Guest out of it. "He is not an obstacle in the mechanism. He is one of its parts. A part that you force is no longer the part you needed."

On her fourth replay Anselm Rook did not come down. He stayed at the bedside, holding a hand that was still warm in a day that would end, and the landing was empty at nine and a half minutes past four. At eleven minutes and twenty seconds past, the horn blew. The Guest lit its lantern and the flame was blue. It offered the lamp to Cato Brandt, who put his hands behind his back.

So the lamp was not Anselm's. It had its own sequence, white and amber and green and blue, and its own unfinished errand, and what the sequence meant, Illuminara found in the ferry's signal book, in a column nobody had read for fifty years. A white lamp on the Ostrel ferry meant ship's business. Amber meant a passenger's. Green meant a child's. Blue meant for the next of kin. The Guest had not been trying people at random. It had been narrowing its address, a passenger, a child, a child's message for the next of kin, and the fourth lamp said all of it at once.

04 · Twenty Seconds

"It knows the whole hour," Wren said. She had come down to the landing for the fifth replay, against Illuminara's advice, and she held her own watch as though it might be taken from her. "So why can it never finish handing over a lamp?"

"Because there are twenty seconds it cannot see," said Illuminara.

She had it from the page of marks. The Guest's hour ran to ten past five, but its attention sealed at nine minutes and forty seconds past, because that was the point at which it began turning back toward the start. In its own sense it perceived every moment of the hour except the last twenty seconds, when the hour was already ending. Those twenty seconds belonged only to ordinary time. Only someone living forward through them could see what happened in them.

What happened was Cato Brandt. At nine minutes and forty-five seconds past five, by the ferry's standing order of 1911, the ferrymaster struck every light on the landing hook that was not the ferry's own, so that nothing foreign hung on the rail when the boat cast off at ten past. Each Tuesday, whoever had taken the Guest's lantern had set it down on the landing, and each Tuesday Cato had picked it up and snuffed it, in the gap between what the Guest could see and what the clock could. He did not remember. The ledger said so every week in the same hand: foreign lamp struck.

"It was never refused," said Illuminara. "It was put out. The handoff happened, and then the hour ended with the lantern in the one pair of hands that would put it out before the hour did."

The Guest heard this and was silent for what might have been a minute or an afternoon. Then it said it was glad someone lived forward, and that it had been wrong about the nature of the failure for a very long time, and that it would like very much not to be right in this way again.

05 · Wednesday

Her sixth Tuesday was arranged around three refusals, and Illuminara asked for each of them and was granted two. Cato Brandt, who distrusted anything that stopped a clock, said he would not bend the standing order for a stranger, and then he heard that the order was what had put out the lamp. He bent it himself. He would hang the Guest's lantern on the hook as the ferry's departure lamp, in his own hands, at five past five, and the ferry would cast off with it burning and cross the river and come back. That was his offer, and his terms, and no one on the council had the authority to alter them.

Anselm Rook would not come down. He said that if he went down he would take the lamp, and that if he took the lamp he would have to read what his daughter had said, and that he would not read it while she was alive on the floor above him. He said he would sit with her until she stopped, and that he would not be hurried and would not be voted on. He asked that the lantern cross the river and the night and be given to him on Wednesday, at dusk, at his kitchen door, by whoever could be trusted to knock. Then he went upstairs and shut the door.

Halse took off her glasses and put the motion into her coat. "Who is to knock?"

"Cato," said Illuminara. "He is the one who carries it past the seam."

The ferry cast off at ten past five with a blue lamp swinging from the hook. The seam came, and nothing snapped back. The market clock on the riverside struck six in the evening, which it had not done in nine Tuesdays, and then seven, and then, a long time after anyone had expected it, it kept going. Pia Rook died at twenty past eight with her father's hand around hers, and the infirmary window looked out on a landing that for once had no one standing at the rail.

On Wednesday the school bell rang for a Wednesday. The red streets on Halse's map went grey one by one, though some of them stayed faint for weeks: a woman who still woke up not sure which day it was, a baker who forgot his own loaves twice a month. At dusk Cato Brandt walked up to a kitchen door in the hill district with a lantern, knocked, and said nothing. Anselm Rook opened the door and looked at the Guest, who stood behind the ferryman in the dusk, and for a long moment he did not hold out his hand. Then he did.

The Guest put the lantern down in his hands. It spoke its first sentence to the end, without a horn to cut it, and said what a child had asked it to carry across a river that was not water, and the man at the door listened with his eyes shut. Then the Guest walked back down the hill and stopped being the Guest of the Ninth Bridge. It was only a visitor on a road, in a town that had gone on to Thursday.

THE DAY THAT KEPT COMING BACK

In the river town of Ostrel one Tuesday keeps restarting at its edges, and what is replaying it is not an enemy but a visitor who cannot see the last twenty seconds of its worst hour. Illuminara has to find those seconds, and a father has to decide what he will and will not be given.

01 · The Market Clock

On the ninth Tuesday the market clock in Ostrel struck six, and the stallholders on the riverside of the square began to unpack the same crates they had unpacked an hour before. Nobody on the hill side noticed at first. The hill side was moving toward Wednesday as it always had, a little late, a little thin, like a coat with the lining worn through.

Illuminara came down the Varna road at noon by the hill's clock and found a town of three thousand one hundred people standing on two different days. At the ferry landing, the school and the market it was early morning again. In the streets between it was afternoon, and in the streets beyond that the afternoons had begun to stutter: a baker whose oven went cold between one loaf and the next, a woman who walked into her kitchen and could not say which day she had left it.

Councillor Mira Halse met her at the toll bridge with a map on which the edge districts were inked in red. "We have a visitor," she said. "The Guest of the Ninth Bridge. It has stood there since the first Tuesday and says it is sorry. Every time it comes back the red grows by a street. We have been moving people up the hill, and the hill has beds for nine hundred, and the ones we moved still wake at six not sure of the day." She folded the map. "The council meets at four. I mean to put a motion to end this, by whatever vote it takes. I would rather put yours."

"Keep moving them," said Illuminara. "It buys you days, and it is right. It cannot end this: three thousand one hundred will not fit in nine hundred beds, and the red does not wait for the beds." She asked to see the Guest first, and she asked one other thing. She had Wren Odira stay on the hill side with a stopwatch and a ledger, because someone had to keep ordinary time while she stood in the loop's. A watch that has never been inside an hour cannot be talked out of what it says.

02 · Three Replays

On the way down she passed the ferry office, where the notices hung under glass: fares, the river tables, and Standing Order 11 of 1911, which required the landing hook to be kept clear at casting-off. She read it the way one reads weather, and went on.

The Guest stood at the rail of the Ninth Bridge landing, tall as a ferry mast and dressed in a ferryman's coat that did not quite agree with its edges. It saw her at once, and all at once: it said that she had arrived, was arriving, and had left by the toll road, and that it hoped none of these was a discourtesy. It perceived the whole of time the way a person perceives a room. It could not walk out of the one hour in which it had failed.

The hour ran from ten past four to ten past five, and the Guest told her its rule in the plain way a ferry states its fares. An errand like its own ended only if the thing carried was still burning, when the hour closed, in the keeping of someone who had lived through the last of it; then the hour closed behind it and did not open again. Otherwise, at ten past five, the edge districts snapped back to six in the morning, and it began again at ten past four with the same weight in its hands and the same shame. It could see its other Tuesdays as one sees other rooms through open doors. It had tried them all, and could not make any of them different from the inside.

Illuminara took three replays to read it. She did not argue with the Guest or comfort it. She had carried a pocket watch and a notebook down the road from Wednesday, and when she asked why they alone were not put back at each snap, the Guest said that an hour returns only what it made, and it had not made them. By the third replay she had a page of marks.

Two things never changed. At 16:10:30 Anselm Rook came down the infirmary stair beside the ferry office and stood at the landing rail, because his daughter Pia was upstairs in the infirmary bed, nine years old, with a heart that would stop that evening, and he could not breathe in the room. And at 16:11:20 the ferry horn blew its warning and covered the Guest's first sentence, which began, I have been carrying.

Two things changed each time. The Guest lit a ferry lantern, and its flame was white on her first replay, amber on her second, green on her third: the four signal lamps in their round, for ship's business, a passenger, a child, the next of kin. And it offered the lantern to someone different. First to Anselm, who looked at it as a man looks at a lit window in a house he will not enter, and climbed the stair. Then to the ferrymaster, Cato Brandt, who put his hands behind his back. Then to Pia's teacher, Joss Maren, who carried it as far as the infirmary door, where Anselm shut the door on both of them. Each time the end was the same: the lantern went on the landing hook, hung there by the Guest after two refusals and by Joss after her failure, because a ferry lamp has nowhere else to go.

Joss lingered by the hook after she had hung it. "Pia used to answer its light from the window with a candle," she said. "For months. She told me she had asked it to carry something to her father, for after. She would not say what."

"It tries someone new each time," Illuminara told Wren afterward, by the hill's clock. "That is what a message looks like when it cannot find the person it was meant for."

03 · The Man Who Stayed Upstairs

"It could be me," Anselm Rook said. He had come out to the toll bridge at dusk, and he spoke to the river rather than to her. "Isn't that what you're thinking? That I am making it. That the lamp is only my grief, standing in a coat."

"It is the best rival I have," said Illuminara. "A lantern made of your grief would follow you. When you were present it would show you; if you left, it would change or go out. I have a prediction, and it can embarrass me. On the fourth replay the flame will be blue, whether or not you come down the stair." She wrote it on a card and gave it to Halse, who had come out behind him with the council's motion in her hand, because Halse stood on the hill side of the hour and a card there could not be put back.

He did not answer for a while. "You want me to stay upstairs."

"I want to know whether you will. It is your hour with her, and you may spend it as you like. I will not ask twice."

Halse said what she had been waiting to say, and it was not small. If ending the loop needed one man's hour, then three thousand one hundred people could vote to take it; his grief was not a veto over everyone else's days, and she had buried enough of the town's Tuesdays already. "I do not yet know what ends it," said Illuminara. "I know that a part you force is no longer the part you needed, and that if I am right and he is not the lock, you will have taken a father's last evening for nothing. Hold the vote until the card is read." Halse looked at the card and then at the man at the rail. "Until the card is read," she said.

On her fourth replay Anselm Rook did not come down. He stayed at the bedside, holding a hand that was still warm in a day that would end, and the landing was empty at 16:10:30. At 16:11:20 the horn blew. The Guest lit its lantern and the flame was blue. It offered the lamp to Cato Brandt, who put his hands behind his back, and then it hung the lamp on the hook.

On the hill side Halse read the card against Illuminara's notebook: blue, Anselm absent, Cato, the hook. She read it twice. "Then it is not his," she said, and put the motion into her coat. She did not call off the evacuation. "Both," she said. "You do not choose which of my plans I trust."

04 · Twenty Seconds

"It knows the whole hour," Wren said at the toll bridge, holding her stopwatch as though it might be taken from her. "So why can it never finish handing over a lamp?"

Illuminara went back down and asked the Guest a different question. Not what it was carrying, but what it saw last. "The lamp on the hook, burning," said the Guest. "The ferrymaster looking at the river. Then I am at the beginning." By her pocket watch the Guest's sight closed at 17:09:40. The snap came at 17:10:00. For twenty seconds, then, there was an hour the Guest did not see, and only someone living forward through it could say what happened in it.

For four replays she had watched the Guest. On the fifth she watched the hook. Wren had come down to the ferry office door against her advice, and the white lantern hung on the hook with nobody near it. At 17:09:40 the Guest's gaze closed like a door. At 17:09:45, a quarter-minute before casting off, Cato Brandt walked along the hook, put out the lantern with two fingers and the flat of his glove, as he put out every light that was not the ferry's own at every casting-off, by the standing order of 1911, and stood looking at the river. He did not remember doing it. At 17:10:00 the town went back to six in the morning, and only Illuminara knew what he had done.

She told the Guest on her sixth replay, at the rail. "Three people turned your lantern down and one could not deliver it, and none of that is what ended your hour badly. The hook did. Whatever happened to the lantern before, it hung there alone in the twenty seconds you cannot see, and the ferryman whose order is to keep that hook clear put it out. You have been judging an errand that ends in a place your sight does not reach."

The Guest heard this and was silent for what might have been a minute or an afternoon. Then it said it was glad someone lived forward, and that it had been wrong about the nature of its failure for a very long time. It had thought it was being refused. It would like very much not to be right in that way again.

05 · Wednesday

Her seventh replay was arranged around three requests, and she was granted two of them whole and the third on someone else's terms. She asked the Guest to light blue on purpose, first, instead of waiting for the round to come to it, because blue was the lamp for the next of kin and the errand had never been meant for anyone else. The Guest, which had reached blue only on every fourth Tuesday, lit it before anything else.

She asked Cato Brandt, who distrusted anything that stopped a clock, to keep the lantern past the seam, and he said he would not bend the standing order for a stranger. Then he heard that the order was what had put the lamp out. He bent it himself, on his own terms. He would hang the Guest's lantern on the hook as the ferry's own departure lamp, which the order allowed him to do, and stand beside it through the quarter-minute with his hand on the hook; the ferry would cast off with it burning, cross the river and come back. No one on the council had the authority to alter his terms, and none tried.

The third request she did not win. She asked Anselm Rook to come down and take the lantern at the landing, and he said that if he took the lamp he would have to read what his daughter had said, and that he would not read it while she was alive on the floor above him. He would sit with her until she stopped, and he would not be hurried and would not be voted on. He asked that the lantern cross the river and the night and be given to him on Wednesday, at dusk, at his kitchen door, by whoever could be trusted to knock. Then he went upstairs and shut the door.

Halse took off her glasses. "Who is to knock?"

"Cato," said Illuminara. "He is the one who carries it past the seam."

The ferry cast off at ten past five with a blue lamp swinging from the hook and Cato Brandt's hand beside it. The seam came, and nothing snapped back. The market clock on the riverside struck six in the evening, which it had not done in nine Tuesdays, and then seven, and then, a long time after anyone had expected it, it kept going. Pia Rook died at twenty past eight with her father's hand around hers, and the infirmary window looked out on a landing that for once had no one standing at the rail.

On Wednesday the school bell rang for a Wednesday. The red streets on Halse's map went grey one by one, though some of them stayed faint for weeks: a woman who still woke up not sure which day it was, a baker who forgot his own loaves twice a month, and families on the hill who went home slowly and not all at once. At dusk Cato Brandt walked up to a kitchen door in the hill district with a lantern, knocked, and said nothing. Anselm Rook opened the door and looked at the Guest, who stood behind the ferryman in the dusk, and for a long moment he did not hold out his hand. Then he did.

The Guest put the lantern down in his hands. It spoke its first sentence to the end, without a horn to cut it, and said what a child had asked it to carry across a river that was not water, and the man at the door listened with his eyes shut. Then the Guest walked back down the hill and stopped being the Guest of the Ninth Bridge. It was only a visitor on a road, in a town that had gone on to Thursday.

DAN, KI SE JE VEDNO ZNOVA VRAČAL

01 · Tržna ura

Devetega torka je tržna ura v Ostrelu odbila šest in prodajalci na rečni strani trga so začeli razkladati iste zaboje, ki so jih razložili uro prej. Na hribovski strani sprva ni nihče opazil. Hribovska stran se je pomikala proti sredi, kakor se je vedno, nekoliko pozna, nekoliko tanka, kakor plašč z izrabljeno podlogo.

Illuminara je po varnski cesti prišla navzdol opoldne po hribovski uri in našla mesto s tri tisoč sto prebivalci, ki je stalo v dveh različnih dneh. Pri trajektnem pristanu, šoli in na trgu je bilo znova zgodnje jutro. Na ulicah vmes je bilo popoldne, na ulicah onkraj njih pa so popoldnevi že začeli jecljati: peku je peč ugasnila med enim hlebcem in naslednjim, ženska je stopila v svojo kuhinjo in ni znala povedati, katerega dne jo je zapustila.

Svetnica Mira Halse jo je pričakala pri mostu z mitnino z zemljevidom, na katerem so bila robna okrožja označena z rdečo. "Imamo obiskovalca," je rekla. "Gosta z Devetega mostu. Tam stoji od prvega torka in pravi, da mu je žal. Vsakič, ko se vrne, se rdeča razširi za eno ulico. Ljudi selimo na hrib, vendar je tam le devetsto postelj, preseljeni pa se še vedno zbujajo ob šestih in ne vedo, kateri dan je." Zložila je zemljevid. "Svet zaseda ob štirih. Predlagala bom, da se to konča, ne glede na potrebno glasovanje. Raje bi predlagala vašo rešitev."

"Še naprej jih selite," je rekla Illuminara. "S tem pridobivate dneve in prav je tako. Končati pa tega ne more: tri tisoč sto ljudi ne gre v devetsto postelj in rdeča ne čaka na postelje." Najprej je želela videti Gosta, prosila pa je še za nekaj. Wren Odira naj ostane na hribovski strani s štoparico in knjigo zapisov, ker je moral nekdo meriti običajni čas, medtem ko bo ona stala v času zanke. Ure, ki še nikoli ni bila znotraj neke ure, ni mogoče pregovoriti, da bi spremenila svoj izkaz.

02 · Tri ponovitve

Na poti navzdol je šla mimo trajektnega urada, kjer so pod steklom visela obvestila: cenik, rečne tablice in Stalna odredba 11 iz leta 1911, ki je zahtevala, da mora biti pristajalni kavelj ob odhodu trajekta prazen. Prebrala jo je, kakor človek prebere vreme, in šla naprej.

Gost je stal pri ograji pristana Devetega mostu, visok kakor trajektni jambor in oblečen v brodarski plašč, ki se ni povsem ujemal z lastnimi robovi. Zagledal jo je takoj in v vseh trenutkih hkrati: rekel je, da je prispela, da prihaja in da je odšla po cesti z mitnino, ter upal, da nič od tega ni nevljudno. Ves čas je zaznaval tako, kakor človek zaznava sobo. Ni mogel stopiti iz ene same ure, v kateri mu je spodletelo.

Ura je tekla od deset čez štiri do deset čez pet in Gost ji je svoje pravilo povedal tako preprosto, kakor trajekt objavi cene. Opravek, kakršen je bil njegov, se je končal le, če je stvar, ki jo je nosil, ob zaprtju ure še gorela v varstvu nekoga, ki je preživel njen konec; tedaj se je ura za njim zaprla in se ni več odprla. Sicer so robna okrožja ob deset čez pet preskočila nazaj na šest zjutraj, on pa je ob deset čez štiri začel znova z isto težo v rokah in istim sramom. Druge torke je videl tako, kakor človek skozi odprta vrata vidi druge sobe. Poskusil jih je vse, vendar nobenega od znotraj ni mogel narediti drugačnega.

Illuminara je najprej postavila očitno vprašanje: ali je koga preprosto prosil, naj drži plamen tudi po deset čez pet? Gost je odgovoril tako, kakor geometer odgovori o meji. Vsak torek je bil že doživeta soba, je rekel. Lahko je stal v njej in odgovoril vsakomur, ki je vstopil od zunaj te ure. Toda ljudje znotraj so lahko sledili le vejam, ki so jih že živeli, in nihče znotraj še ni živel veje, v kateri bi svetilka prečkala šiv. Lahko je opazoval, kaj se dogaja v sobi. Znotraj sobe pa ni mogel začeti nečesa, česar soba še nikoli ni vsebovala.

Illuminara je potrebovala tri ponovitve, da ga je prebrala. Z Gostom se ni prepirala in ga ni tolažila. Iz srede je po cesti prinesla žepno uro in zvezek; ko ga je vprašala, zakaj se ob vsakem preskoku le onadva ne povrneta, je Gost rekel, da ura vrne le tisto, kar je sama ustvarila, njiju pa ni. Do tretje ponovitve je imela stran znamenj.

Dve stvari se nista nikoli spremenili. Ob 16:10:30 je Anselm Rook prišel po stopnicah ambulante ob trajektnem uradu in obstal pri ograji pristana, ker je njegova devetletna hči Pia ležala zgoraj v bolniški postelji s srcem, ki se bo tisti večer ustavilo, on pa v sobi ni mogel dihati. Ob 16:11:20 je trajektna hupa zatrobila opozorilo in preglasila Gostov prvi stavek, ki se je začel: Nosim.

Dve stvari sta se vsakič spremenili. Gost je prižgal trajektno svetilko in njen plamen je bil ob vsaki ponovitvi druge barve: prvič bel, drugič jantarjev, tretjič zelen. To so bile štiri signalne svetilke v njegovem krogu in vsaka je pripovedovala eno plast opravka, ki ga je nosil: ladijsko zadevo, prečkanje, otroka, najbližjega sorodnika. Barva je povedala, kateri del opravka se pripoveduje, ne kdo ga posluša. Tudi svetilka je vsakič šla drugemu. Najprej Anselmu, ki jo je pogledal tako, kakor človek gleda osvetljeno okno hiše, v katero ne bo stopil, nato pa se je povzpel po stopnicah. Potem brodarju Catu Brandtu, ki je sklenil roke za hrbtom. Nato Piini učiteljici Joss Maren, ki jo je prinesla do vrat ambulante, kjer je Anselm obema zaprl vrata. Konec je bil vsakič enak: svetilka je pristala na pristajalnem kavlju; Gost jo je obesil po dveh zavrnitvah, Joss pa po svojem neuspehu, ker trajektna svetilka ni imela kam drugam.

Joss je po tem, ko jo je obesila, še postala ob kavlju. "Pia se je na njeno svetlobo z okna odzivala s svečo," je rekla. "Več mesecev. Povedala mi je, da jo je prosila, naj nekaj odnese njenemu očetu, za potem. Ni hotela povedati kaj."

"Vsakič poskusi z nekom novim," je Illuminara pozneje povedala Wren ob hribovski uri. "Tako je videti sporočilo, ki ne more najti človeka, kateremu je namenjeno."

03 · Mož, ki je ostal zgoraj

"Lahko sem jaz," je rekel Anselm Rook. Ob mraku je prišel k mostu z mitnino in govoril reki namesto njej. "Ali ne razmišljate prav o tem? Da to ustvarjam jaz. Da je svetilka samo moja žalost, oblečena v plašč."

"To je najboljša tekmujoča razlaga, ki jo imam," je rekla Illuminara. "Svetilka, narejena iz vaše žalosti, bi vam sledila. Ko bi bili navzoči, bi kazala vas; če bi odšli, bi se spremenila ali ugasnila. Barve so Gostov lastni krog, krog pa ne ve, kje stojite. Imam napoved in ta me lahko osramoti. Ob četrti ponovitvi bo plamen moder, ne glede na to, ali boste prišli po stopnicah." Zapisala jo je na kartico in jo dala Halse, ki je prišla za njim s predlogom sveta v roki, kajti Halse je stala na hribovski strani ure in kartice tam ni bilo mogoče povrniti.

Nekaj časa ni odgovoril. "Hočete, da ostanem zgoraj."

"Hočem vedeti, ali boste. To je vaša ura z njo in lahko jo preživite, kakor želite. Ne bom vprašala dvakrat."

Halse je izrekla, kar je čakala, da bo lahko povedala, in ni bilo malo. Če je bil za konec zanke potreben en človekov čas, je lahko tri tisoč sto ljudi glasovalo, da mu ga vzame; njegova žalost ni bila veto nad dnevi vseh drugih, ona pa je pokopala že dovolj mestnih torkov. "Ne vem še, kaj jo konča," je rekla Illuminara. "Vem pa, da vloga, ki jo izsilite, ni več vloga, ki ste jo potrebovali, in če imam prav ter on ni ključavnica, boste očetu zaman vzeli zadnji večer. Zadržite glasovanje, dokler kartica ne bo prebrana." Halse je pogledala kartico in nato moža pri ograji. "Dokler kartica ne bo prebrana," je rekla.

Ob njeni četrti ponovitvi Anselm Rook ni prišel navzdol. Ostal je ob postelji in držal roko, ki je bila še topla v dnevu, ki se bo končal, pristanišče pa je bilo ob 16:10:30 prazno. Ob 16:11:20 je zatrobila hupa. Gost je prižgal svetilko in plamen je bil moder. Ponudil jo je Catu Brandtu, ki je sklenil roke za hrbtom, nato pa jo je obesil na kavelj.

Na hribovski strani je Halse kartico primerjala z Illuminarinim zvezkom: modra, Anselm odsoten, Cato, kavelj. Prebrala jo je dvakrat. "Potem ni njegovo," je rekla in pospravila predlog v plašč. Evakuacije ni preklicala. "Oboje," je rekla. "Vi ne odločate, kateremu od svojih načrtov bom zaupala."

04 · Dvajset sekund

"Pozna vso uro," je rekla Wren pri mostu z mitnino in držala štoparico, kakor da bi ji jo lahko kdo odvzel. "Zakaj mu torej nikoli ne uspe dokončati predaje svetilke?"

Illuminara se je vrnila navzdol in Gostu postavila drugačno vprašanje. Ne kaj nosi, temveč kaj vidi zadnje. "Svetilko na kavlju, prižgano," je rekel Gost. "Brodarja, ki gleda reko. Potem sem na začetku." Po njeni žepni uri se je Gostov vid zaprl ob 17:09:40. Preskok je prišel ob 17:10:00. Dvajset sekund je torej obstajala ura, ki je Gost ni videl, in le nekdo, ki jo je živel naprej, je lahko povedal, kaj se je v njej zgodilo.

Štiri ponovitve je opazovala Gosta. Ob peti je opazovala kavelj. Wren je v nasprotju z njenim nasvetom prišla do vrat trajektnega urada, bela svetilka pa je visela na kavlju brez kogar koli v bližini. Ob 17:09:40 se je Gostov pogled zaprl kakor vrata. Ob 17:09:45, petnajst sekund pred odhodom, je Cato Brandt stopil h kavlju in z dvema prstoma ter ploskvijo rokavice ugasnil svetilko, kakor je ob vsakem odhodu po Stalni odredbi iz leta 1911 ugasnil vsako luč, ki ni pripadala trajektu, nato pa je obstal in gledal reko. Tega se ni spominjal. Ob 17:10:00 se je mesto vrnilo na šest zjutraj in le Illuminara je vedela, kaj je storil.

Ob šesti ponovitvi je to povedala Gostu pri ograji. "Trije ljudje so vašo svetilko zavrnili in eni je ni uspelo dostaviti, vendar nič od tega ni povzročilo slabega konca vaše ure. Povzročil ga je kavelj. Ne glede na to, kaj se je prej zgodilo s svetilko, je v dvajsetih sekundah, ki jih ne vidite, sama visela tam, brodar, čigar pravilo zahteva prazen kavelj, pa jo je ugasnil. Presojali ste opravek, ki se konča na kraju, kamor vaš vid ne seže."

Gost je to slišal in molčal tako dolgo, da bi lahko minila minuta ali popoldne. Nato je rekel, da je vesel, da je nekdo živel naprej, in da se je zelo dolgo motil o naravi svojega neuspeha. Mislil je, da ga zavračajo. Rekel je tudi, da tega sam ne bi mogel odkriti: njegov vid se je končal, preden se je kdo dotaknil kavlja, vsaka soba, v katero je lahko stopil, pa je bila soba, v kateri je bil kavelj že zapuščen. Zelo si je želel, da mu na tak način ne bi bilo treba biti nikoli več prepričan v svoj prav.

05 · Sreda

Njena sedma ponovitev je bila urejena okoli treh prošenj; dve sta ji bili izpolnjeni v celoti, tretja pa pod pogoji nekoga drugega. Gosta je prosila, naj namerno najprej prižge modro, namesto da čaka, da v krogu pride na vrsto, kajti modra je bila plast za najbližjega sorodnika, opravek pa ni bil nikoli namenjen nikomur drugemu. Gost, ki bi ga krog pripeljal do zelene, je modro prižgal pred vsem drugim. Barve so ji povedale le, čigav del opravka je bil na vrsti, in nikoli ni trdila ničesar več.

Cata Brandta, ki ni zaupal ničemur, kar ustavi uro, je prosila, naj svetilko ohrani čez šiv; rekel je, da zaradi tujca ne bo uklonil stalne odredbe. V roke mu je položila stran svojih znamenj: 17:09:45, njegova rokavica, kavelj. Nato je slišal, da je svetilko ugasnila prav odredba. Uklonil jo je sam in pod lastnimi pogoji. Gostovo svetilko bo obesil na kavelj kot trajektno odhodno luč, kar mu je odredba dovoljevala, ter vseh petnajst sekund stal ob njej z roko na kavlju; trajekt bo odplul s prižgano lučjo, prečkal reko in se vrnil. Nihče v svetu ni imel pooblastila, da bi spreminjal njegove pogoje, in nihče ni poskusil.

Tretje prošnje ni dobila. Anselma Rooka je prosila, naj pride navzdol in na pristanu vzame svetilko, on pa je rekel, da bi moral, če jo vzame, prebrati, kaj je povedala njegova hči, tega pa ne bo prebral, dokler je še živa v nadstropju nad njim. Sedel bo ob njej, dokler se ne ustavi; ne bo dovolil, da ga priganjajo, in o njem se ne bo glasovalo. Prosil je, naj svetilka prečka reko in noč ter mu jo v sredo ob mraku pri kuhinjskih vratih izroči nekdo, ki mu je mogoče zaupati, da bo potrkal. Nato je šel gor in zaprl vrata.

Halse je snela očala. "Kdo bo potrkal?"

"Cato," je rekla Illuminara. "On jo bo ponesel čez šiv."

Trajekt je odplul ob deset čez pet z modro svetilko, ki je nihala na kavlju, in roko Cata Brandta ob njej. Prišel je šiv in nič ni preskočilo nazaj. Tržna ura na rečni strani je odbila šest zvečer, česar ni storila že devet torkov, nato sedem, potem pa je dolgo po času, ko so vsi pričakovali konec, še vedno tekla. Pia Rook je umrla ob dvajset čez osem z očetovo roko okoli svoje, okno ambulante pa je gledalo na pristan, kjer ob ograji prvič ni stal nihče.

V sredo je šolski zvonec zazvonil za sredo. Rdeče ulice na zemljevidu Halse so druga za drugo postale sive, čeprav so nekatere ostale šibke še več tednov: ženska se je še vedno zbujala, ne da bi vedela, kateri dan je, pek je dvakrat na mesec pozabil svoje hlebce, družine s hriba pa so se domov vračale počasi in ne vse hkrati. Ob mraku je Cato Brandt s svetilko prišel do kuhinjskih vrat v hribovskem okrožju, potrkal in ni rekel ničesar. Anselm Rook je odprl vrata in pogledal Gosta, ki je stal za brodarjem v mraku, ter dolgo ni iztegnil roke. Nato jo je.

Gost mu je položil svetilko v roke. Svoj prvi stavek je izgovoril do konca, ne da bi ga prekinila hupa, in povedal, kaj ga je otrok prosil prenesti čez reko, ki ni bila voda; mož pri vratih je poslušal z zaprtimi očmi. Zatem je Gost reki povedal, da veja, v kateri je svetilka prečkala šiv, ni bila veja, ki bi jo bil kdor koli znotraj dneva že živel, in je sam ni mogel začeti: iz srede je prišla na štoparici, ohranil pa jo je mož, ki je uklonil lastno pravilo. Nato je Gost odšel po hribu navzdol in ni bil več Gost z Devetega mostu. Bil je le obiskovalec na cesti v mestu, ki je nadaljevalo v četrtek.
