THE DOOR IN THE BAKERY

A cold-room door opens on a second kitchen, and the town's clocks, radios and geese have begun to slip. Illuminara has until noon, a ministry with a block of concrete, and two bakers who will not be made to give the door up.

01 · Loaf for Loaf

At twenty to six on the seventh morning, Orsa Keller unlatched the cold-room door of her bakery in Kesselbach and found a second kitchen on the other side, as she had every morning that week. The flour in there was the same flour. The oven burned a quiet blue, and the dough on its board had finished rising while she was still saying good morning.

"Loaf for loaf," said Tem, who baked there, and passed one through the frame. It was enormous and weighed almost nothing: crust like a paper lantern, crumb full of holes. Orsa gave him her rye in return, close-grained, the size of a brick, and he grunted as he caught it. That was the trade they had made on the first day. They had kept it fourteen times, and it had felt like the fairest thing in either world.

Outside, the town was coming apart at the edges. The radios had been slipping off their stations since Tuesday, so that the morning news turned up between the dial marks and then nowhere. The church clock gained until Old Josef, who wound it, stopped apologizing for it. And a hundred and forty grey geese, bound south, had circled the square for three days and would not come down.

The Ministry of Standards had pinned a notice to the bakery's front door: the premises would be sealed with poured concrete at noon. Tem had one too, from the Hearth Guild, with a different seal and a different noon. He had stuck it on his wall beside a drawing of a goose.

"They both want the door," Tem said.

"They want the bread," said Orsa. "The door is just where it comes out."

She had written to the one person she thought might know how to look at a thing without owning it. Illuminara arrived at six with Wren Odira, who carried a stopwatch and the expression of someone who had volunteered to keep the clock because nobody else would.

02 · A Number It Can Embarrass

"Don't tell me what's wrong yet," Illuminara said, before anyone could. "Shut it and latch it, and let me measure. I will say what I think once there is a number it can embarrass."

Inspector Dalia Pryce of the Ministry had brought a frequency counter in a leather case and the look of a woman who was used to being blamed for the weather. For an hour the door stayed shut. Wren read the church clock against her stopwatch every ten minutes: four minutes gained, to the second, steady as a pulse. Pryce's counter put the town's station nine point six kilohertz low and sliding another one point four every ten minutes. "A closed door, and it still slides," said Pryce. "Residue. Seal it and it decays." She wrote that down as her theory, and she wrote the numbers first.

Illuminara laid her palm on the frame. Force paths were legible to her the way a tide is to a sailor, and this one showed a slow, patient lean toward the other kitchen, like a held breath. What the lean meant was not handed to her, and she said so. "It leans. I do not know whether the lean is the sickness or only its shadow. Tell me everything that crossed, each way."

"Fourteen loaves each way," said Orsa. She had it chalked on a flour sack. "Loaf for loaf."

"Weigh them."

Orsa owned a balance with brass weights, older than the bakery. She set a rye on one pan and brought the weights down to nine hundred and twelve grams. Tem passed one of his through the frame. She set it on the pan: four hundred and eight.

Nobody spoke. Tem looked at his loaf as if it had lied to him. "But a loaf is a loaf."

"Not to a scale," said Illuminara. "Five hundred and four grams a trade, fourteen trades. Seven kilos have left her side and gone into yours."

She did not announce the cause. She took the chalk and wrote two sentences on the sack. The first: If the town is reading a standing balance, then a lopsided trade of three kilos back toward Orsa should cut the slide from 1.4 to 0.8 kilohertz per ten minutes, and the clock's gain from four minutes an hour to about two and a third, after a delay I cannot yet name. The second: If it does not, I am wrong.

"I have a rival," said Pryce. "An open door leaks, however you balance what goes through it. Throughput."

"And I've got another," said Tem. "My loaves come out of a blue oven hot, and yours go in cold. It is the heat."

"Good," said Illuminara. "Then I will need a second trade that can embarrass both of you."

03 · Seven Minutes

The door, Orsa had learned on the third night, opened only for two hands. Alone, with her palm on the latch, she found a cold room with potatoes in it; with Tem's palm on his side, a doorway. Nobody had designed it that way. It meant that nothing had ever crossed that both of them had not chosen to send.

"If I send you four kilos and take one back," Tem said, "my side gets lighter. My dough rises in forty seconds now. The Guild's kettles boil when they please. Light is how I became fast, and I don't know what I am without it."

"Slower, probably," said Illuminara. "I don't know. It is yours to choose."

Tem looked at his blue oven. "My grandmother said a dough you needn't wait for is a dough nobody's learned anything from." He put his hand on the latch. "Go on, then."

At twelve minutes past seven the last loaf crossed: ten of Tem's hollow loaves for Orsa's rye and a roll, four kilos against one, a little over three net toward Orsa's side. Wren started the watch.

Four minutes: nothing. Pryce stood over her counter with her arms folded, the way people stand over a pot. Five. Six. At seven minutes the counter stuttered like a man changing his mind and settled on zero point eight. Across the square Josef's bell struck the quarter, and Wren, checking, said, "The clock has slowed. Not stopped. Two and a bit an hour."

"Don't look at me like that," said Pryce. "I'll sign the sheet. But it is one number, and one number is a coincidence with good manners."

"Then take the next," said Illuminara. "Seven minutes is how long the door takes to answer. I could not have told you that this morning. Now it is something to predict with."

Tem had weighed his ten loaves on his own side's spring scale before sending them, and chalked 3.7 kilos. Orsa's brass weights had said 4.08. He stared at the two numbers for a while. "Springs lie across a door," he said slowly. "My grandmother never trusted one. We weighed the First Oven's grain on a beam." He was already reaching for his coat. "The Guild keeps the Charter beam in the hall. I'll be back before the next trade."

04 · The Loud Loaf

He was gone forty minutes. When he came back he had the Charter beam under one arm, two brass pans on a bar of black oak, and behind him in the frame, in a green coat, stood Master Ilse Varro of the Hearth Guild with a writ in her hand.

Tem hung the beam across the doorway so that one pan lay on each side and the pivot sat in the frame itself. "Whatever pulls on a pan, here or there, the pivot is where both kitchens agree. It compares. It does not read."

The second trade was five kilos each way, level to the gram: Orsa's rye, cold from the cellar at six degrees, against twelve loaves and a heel straight from the blue oven, ninety in the crumb. "Ten kilos through the door," said Pryce, "three times the first. Mine says it screams."

"Mine says nothing at all," said Illuminara. "Tem's says the hot ones push the slide the wrong way. Write down who said what."

At twenty-one minutes past eight the last crust crossed. Wren set the watch. At seven minutes, nothing. At fourteen, nothing. The counter sat at zero point eight as if it had been painted there, and the church clock kept its two and a bit.

Pryce took out her pencil and struck one line from her notebook, throughput, and initialed the crossing-out. It was the most expensive thing anyone did all day. "Ten kilos," she said, "and the door doesn't care. Fine. Then seal it anyway. Whatever it is, you have proved it is real."

"Seal it now," said Illuminara, "and you freeze four kilos of deficit into this town for good. The clock gains two minutes an hour for as long as there is concrete. Your own counter will tell you so every morning, and you will not be able to open the door to pay it back."

Varro cleared her throat in the frame. "The Guild has a prior claim. The Charter of the First Oven..."

"Says the oven belongs to whoever feeds the town from it," said Tem. "I read it to you myself when I was your apprentice. Master, it opens for two hands. Take my latch and you hold a cold cupboard in a world with no Orsa in it." He did not raise his voice.

"Same to you, Inspector," said Orsa. "My flour, my door. You are welcome to a gauge. You cannot have the latch."

05 · Level to Fifty Grams

The repayment took the rest of the morning, and it was nobody's miracle. Four times over, Tem sent four and a half loaves for each of Orsa's rye, and the beam leaned a little less each time toward the side that had been giving. At every pair both bakers called the weight aloud, and Wren wrote the running balance on the sack. Illuminara kept out of it, except to read the lean in the frame and say, each time, whether it had eased.

Orsa made the first rule, with her hand on the latch. "Level to fifty grams, or it is chalked on the beam and paid back in the next trade. And we both say the number aloud. If you can't say it, you don't send it." Tem made the second: the ledger stays in the doorway, chalk on oak, where either of them can read it. Illuminara added the only piece she was better placed to see: the town was already reading the balance for free. The clock and the station were the gauges. The Ministry could read the counter, the Guild its kettles. Neither would hold a hand.

Pryce read it twice and said it was irregular, which was true, and that it was also the only arrangement under which she could see the thing she was meant to protect, which was also true. She signed as an observer. Varro, after a long minute in Tem's kitchen, signed with a pen she asked to borrow.

At ten past eleven the deficit stood at two hundred and twenty grams and would go no lower. "That is flour of Orsa's that went through your blue oven," Illuminara told Tem. "It left your chimney as heat and light. A door cannot carry smoke." Orsa laughed until she had to sit down on the flour sack. The clock would gain about three minutes a day. Josef, who had wound it for forty years, said he would put it right on Sundays and was glad, at last, to have a reason.

At eleven forty-eight the counter turned and began to creep back toward its station, a few hundred hertz at a time. The geese noticed before the radios did. One came down in the square, then ten, then all hundred and forty, in a long untidy landing, and went straight for the crusts the bakers' children had brought them.

There were costs. Orsa's rye now travelled in pairs with Tem's airy loaves, and she would bake six hours longer each week. Tem's dough took four minutes to rise, and he complained about it bitterly and happily. The concrete order was withdrawn rather than torn up, and sat in both offices as an unsigned threat.

At noon the radios found their marks. Orsa and Tem stood at the beam in the doorway with a pan each. "Level?" said Tem. "Level to forty," said Orsa. They put their hands on the latches. The door opened for two hands.

THE DOOR IN THE BAKERY

A cold-room door opens on a second kitchen, and the town's clocks, radios and geese have begun to slip. Illuminara has until noon, a ministry with a block of concrete, and two bakers who will not be made to give the door up.

01 · Loaf for Loaf

At twenty to six on the seventh morning, Orsa Keller unlatched the cold-room door of her bakery in Kesselbach and found a second kitchen on the other side, as she had every morning that week. The flour in there was the same flour. The oven burned a quiet blue, and the dough on its board had finished rising while she was still saying good morning.

"Loaf for loaf," said Tem, who baked there, and passed one through the frame. It was enormous and weighed almost nothing: crust like a paper lantern, crumb full of holes. Orsa gave him her rye in return, close-grained, the size of a brick, and he grunted as he caught it. That was the trade they had made on the first day. They had kept it fourteen times, and it had felt like the fairest thing in either world.

Outside, the town was coming apart at the edges. The radios had been slipping off their stations since Tuesday, so that the morning news turned up between the dial marks and then nowhere. The church clock gained until Old Josef, who wound it, stopped apologizing for it. And a hundred and forty grey geese, bound south, had circled the square for three days and would not come down.

The Ministry of Standards had pinned a notice to the bakery's front door: the premises would be sealed with poured concrete at noon. Tem had one too, from the Hearth Guild, with a different seal and a different noon. He had stuck it on his wall beside a drawing of a goose.

"They both want the door," Tem said.

"They want the bread," said Orsa. "The door is just where it comes out."

She had written to the one person she thought might know how to look at a thing without owning it. Illuminara arrived at six with Wren Odira, who carried a stopwatch and the expression of someone who had volunteered to keep the clock because nobody else would.

02 · A Number It Can Embarrass

"Don't tell me what's wrong yet," Illuminara said, before anyone could. "Shut it and latch it, and let me measure. I will say what I think once there is a number it can embarrass."

Inspector Dalia Pryce of the Ministry had brought a frequency counter in a leather case and the look of a woman who was used to being blamed for the weather. For an hour the door stayed shut. Wren read the church clock against her stopwatch every ten minutes: four minutes gained, to the second, steady as a pulse. Pryce's counter put the town's station nine point six kilohertz low and sliding another one point four every ten minutes. "A closed door, and it still slides," said Pryce. "Residue. Seal it and it decays." She wrote that down as her theory, and she wrote the numbers first.

Illuminara laid her palm on the frame. Force paths were legible to her the way a tide is to a sailor, and this one showed a slow, patient lean toward the other kitchen, like a held breath. What the lean meant was not handed to her, and she said so. "It leans. I do not know whether the lean is the sickness or only its shadow. Tell me everything that crossed, each way."

"Fourteen loaves each way," said Orsa. She had it chalked on a flour sack. "Loaf for loaf."

"Weigh them."

Orsa owned a balance with brass weights, older than the bakery. She set a rye on one pan and brought the weights down to nine hundred and twelve grams. Tem passed one of his through the frame. She set it on the pan: four hundred and eight.

Nobody spoke. Tem looked at his loaf as if it had lied to him. "But a loaf is a loaf."

"Not to a scale," said Illuminara. "Five hundred and four grams a trade, fourteen trades. Seven thousand and fifty-six grams have left her side and gone into yours."

She did not announce the cause. She took the chalk and wrote two sentences on the sack. The first: If the town is reading a standing balance, then a net trade of about three kilos back toward Orsa should cut the slide from 1.4 to 0.8 kilohertz per ten minutes, and the clock's gain from four minutes an hour to about two and a quarter, after a delay I cannot yet name. The second: If it does not, I am wrong.

"I have a rival," said Pryce. "An open door leaks, however you balance what goes through it. Throughput."

"And I've got another," said Tem. "My loaves come out of a blue oven hot, and yours go in cold. It is the heat."

"Good," said Illuminara. "Then I will need a second trade that can embarrass both of you."

03 · Seven Minutes

The door, Orsa had learned on the third night, opened only for two hands. Alone, with her palm on the latch, she found a cold room with potatoes in it; with Tem's palm on his side, a doorway. Nobody had designed it that way. It meant that nothing had ever crossed that both of them had not chosen to send.

"If I send you four kilos and take one back," Tem said, "my side gets lighter. My dough rises in forty seconds now. The Guild's kettles boil when they please. Light is how I became fast, and I don't know what I am without it."

"Slower, probably," said Illuminara. "I don't know. It is yours to choose."

Tem looked at his blue oven. "My grandmother said a dough you needn't wait for is a dough nobody's learned anything from." He put his hand on the latch. "Go on, then."

At twelve minutes past seven the last loaf crossed: ten of Tem's hollow loaves, four thousand and eighty grams, against Orsa's rye and a roll, a thousand even. Net three thousand and eighty toward Orsa's side. Wren started the watch.

Four minutes: nothing. Pryce stood over her counter with her arms folded, the way people stand over a pot. Five. Six. At seven minutes the counter stuttered like a man changing his mind and settled on zero point eight. Across the square Josef's bell struck the quarter, and Wren, checking, said, "The clock has slowed. Not stopped. Two and a quarter an hour."

"Don't look at me like that," said Pryce. "I'll sign the sheet. But it is one number, and one number is a coincidence with good manners."

"Then take the next," said Illuminara. "Seven minutes is how long the town takes to feel a change in the balance. I could not have told you that this morning. Now it is something to predict with."

Tem had weighed his ten loaves on his own side's spring scale before sending them, and chalked 3.7 kilos. Orsa's brass weights had said 4.08. He stared at the two numbers for a while. "Springs lie across a door," he said slowly. "My grandmother never trusted one. We weighed the First Oven's grain on a beam." He was already reaching for his coat. "The Guild keeps the Charter beam in the hall. I'll be back before the next trade."

04 · The Loud Loaf

He was gone forty minutes. When he came back he had the Charter beam under one arm, two brass pans on a bar of black oak, and behind him in the frame, in a green coat, stood Master Ilse Varro of the Hearth Guild with a writ in her hand.

Tem hung the beam across the doorway so that one pan lay on each side and the pivot sat in the frame itself. "Whatever pulls on a pan, here or there, the pivot is where both kitchens agree. It compares. It does not read."

The second trade was five thousand grams each way, level to the gram: Orsa's five ryes and a cob of four hundred and forty, cold from the cellar at six degrees, against twelve hollow loaves and a heel of a hundred and four, straight from the blue oven, ninety in the crumb. "Ten kilos through the door," said Pryce, "twice the first. Mine says it screams."

"Mine says nothing at all," said Illuminara. "Tem's says the hot ones push the slide the wrong way. Write down who said what."

At twenty-one minutes past eight the last crust crossed. Wren set the watch. At seven minutes, nothing. At fourteen, nothing. The counter sat at zero point eight as if it had been painted there, and the church clock kept its two and a quarter.

Pryce took out her pencil and struck one line from her notebook, throughput, and initialed the crossing-out. It was the most expensive thing anyone did all day. "Ten kilos," she said, "and the door doesn't care. Fine. Then seal it anyway. Whatever it is, you have proved it is real."

"Seal it now," said Illuminara, "and you freeze nearly four kilos of deficit into this town for good. The clock gains two and a quarter minutes an hour for as long as there is concrete, and the dial goes on sliding with it. Your own counter will tell you so every morning, and you will not be able to open the door to pay it back."

Varro cleared her throat in the frame. "The Guild has a prior claim. The Charter of the First Oven..."

"Says the oven belongs to whoever feeds the town from it," said Tem. "I read it to you myself when I was your apprentice. Master, it opens for two hands. Take my latch and you hold a cold cupboard in a world with no Orsa in it." He did not raise his voice.

"Same to you, Inspector," said Orsa. "My flour, my door. You are welcome to a gauge. You cannot have the latch."

05 · Level to Fifty Grams

The repayment took the rest of the morning, and it was nobody's miracle. Three trades, with both bakers calling every weight aloud and Wren chalking the running balance on the sack. At half past nine Tem sent five hollow loaves against one rye, two thousand and forty against nine hundred and twelve, and the deficit fell from 3,976 grams to 2,848. At a quarter past ten, the same trade again: 1,720. At three minutes past eleven, five loaves against a rye-and-seed cob of three hundred and sixty, and the sack read 40. Each time the counter answered seven minutes later, as it had before, to zero point six, to zero point three, to a flicker. Illuminara kept out of the weighing, except to read the lean in the frame and say, each time, whether it had eased.

Orsa made the first rule, with her hand on the latch. "Level to fifty grams, or it is chalked on the beam and paid back in the next trade. And we both say the number aloud. If you can't say it, you don't send it." Tem made the second: the ledger stays in the doorway, chalk on oak, where either of them can read it. Illuminara added the only piece she was better placed to see: the town was already reading the balance for free. The clock and the station were the gauges. The Ministry could read the counter, the Guild its kettles. Neither would hold a hand.

Pryce read it twice and said it was irregular, which was true, and that it was also the only arrangement under which she could see the thing she was meant to protect, which was also true. Then she asked for one thing. The counter would stand in the square on a post, where any child could read it, and if it ever showed a slide above a quarter of a kilohertz per ten minutes, both bakers would send nothing but repayment until it fell. "A beam can be dropped," she said. "A town cannot be asked to trust a beam." Orsa weighed it and agreed, and wrote beside it that the bakers could cancel it together and the Ministry could not cancel it alone.

Varro had stood a long minute in Tem's kitchen. Then she said the Charter beam had not been checked against the Guild's standard weights in nine years, and that she would not see it carried across a door unproven. She would bring the standards herself each month, with Orsa at the other pan, and either baker could take the beam back from the Guild's keeping the same day. She signed with a pen she asked to borrow. Tem noticed that she had asked for a duty and not a key.

The sack read 40 and would go no lower that morning. At ten past eleven the counter was no longer sliding, and Illuminara said what the morning had shown. "There are two clocks here, and we have been timing only one. The rate follows the balance, seven minutes behind it. That is the one we have tested, and it has answered every time. Where the dial sits is a different quantity. It has its own pace home, and Wren can time it now that the rate has stopped." Wren already was. The station stood twenty-seven and a half kilohertz low and came back at four and a half every ten minutes, a flat steady walk. The geese had begun to circle, her Tuesday notes said, when the dial passed eight low. By her arithmetic they would land at seven minutes to noon.

They landed at eight minutes to. One came down in the square, then ten, then all hundred and forty, in a long untidy landing, and went straight for the crusts the bakers' children had brought them.

There were costs. Orsa's rye now travelled in pairs with Tem's airy loaves, and she would bake six hours longer each week. Tem's dough took four minutes to rise, and he complained about it bitterly and happily. The church clock stood fifty-one minutes fast, and Josef, who had wound it for forty years, put it right with his own hands, which took him an hour and a ladder, and said it was the best hour he had spent that month. Until the forty grams were paid back it would gain half a minute a day. The concrete order was withdrawn rather than torn up, and sat in both offices as an unsigned threat.

At ten past twelve the radios found their marks. Orsa and Tem stood at the beam in the doorway with a pan each. "Level?" said Tem. "Level to forty," said Orsa. They put their hands on the latches. The door opened for two hands.

04 · The Loud Loaf

He was gone forty minutes. When he came back he had the Charter beam under one arm, two brass pans on a bar of black oak, and behind him in the frame, in a green coat, stood Master Ilse Varro of the Hearth Guild with a writ in her hand.

Tem hung the beam across the doorway so that one pan lay on each side and the pivot sat in the frame itself. "Whatever pulls on a pan, here or there, the pivot is where both kitchens agree. It compares. It does not read."

"It compares pulls," said Illuminara, "and your spring has just told us the two kitchens may not agree about what a kilo pulls. Before it weighs a town's bread, show me it is fair." She asked for the plainest test she knew. Orsa set her brass kilo on her pan. Varro, who had not yet spoken, unwrapped the Guild's sealed standard kilo from its felt and set it on Tem's. The beam hung level in the frame. Then the two of them carried the pans across the threshold, brass to Tem and standard to Orsa, and hung them again. Level.

"Had a kilo pulled harder on one side," Illuminara said, "the swap would have tipped it. It did not. The beam is fair, the town's brass is the Guild's kilo, and it is the spring that lies." Tem said a spring remembered the metal of its own world and he had never once thought to ask the other world's opinion. Varro folded the felt over her standard without a word. It was the first thing she had done that morning before objecting to it.

The second trade was five thousand grams each way, level to the gram: Orsa's five ryes and a cob of four hundred and forty, cold from the cellar at six degrees, against twelve hollow loaves and a heel of a hundred and four, straight from the blue oven, ninety in the crumb. 05 · Level to Fifty Grams

The repayment took the rest of the morning, and it was nobody's miracle. Three trades, with both bakers calling every weight aloud and Wren chalking the running balance on the sack. At a quarter past ten, the same trade again: 1,720. At three minutes past eleven, five loaves against a rye-and-seed cob of three hundred and sixty, and the sack read 40. A cob of four hundred would have cleared it, but Orsa had none left, and her oven would not give her one before morning. Each time the counter answered seven minutes later, as it had before, to zero point six, to zero point three, to a flicker. Illuminara kept out of the weighing, except to read the lean in the frame and say, each time, whether it had eased.

Orsa made the first rule, with her hand on the latch. "Level to fifty grams to send. Whatever the sack says under fifty is owed, and the owing goes first through the door in the next crossing, before anything else. And we both say the number aloud. If you can't say it, you don't send it." Tem made the second: the ledger stays in the doorway, chalk on oak, where either of them can read it. Illuminara added the only piece she was better placed to see: the town was already reading the balance for free. The clock and the station were the gauges. The Ministry could read the counter, the Guild its kettles. Neither would hold a hand.

Then she asked for one thing. "A beam can be dropped," she said. "A town cannot be asked to trust a beam." Orsa weighed it and agreed, and wrote beside it that the bakers could cancel it together and the Ministry could not cancel it alone.

Varro had stood a long minute in Tem's kitchen. Then she said that a beam which had been right at eight o'clock would not stay right for ever, that nobody had checked this one in nine years, and that she would not see a town's bread weighed on it unproven. She would bring the standards herself each month, with Orsa at the other pan, and the pans would be swapped as they had been today; either baker could take the beam back from the Guild's keeping the same day. She signed with a pen she asked to borrow. Tem noticed that she had asked for a duty and not a key.

The sack read 40 and stayed there. At ten past eleven the counter was no longer sliding, and Illuminara said what the morning had shown. "There are two clocks here, and we have been timing only one. The forty grams were still owed, and until they were paid the clock would gain half a minute a day. The concrete order was withdrawn rather than torn up, and sat in both offices as an unsigned threat.

At twenty to six the next morning, before the bread, a single roll of exactly forty grams crossed from Tem's side with nothing sent back, and Tem chalked the sack clean. Seven minutes later Wren looked up from her stopwatch and said that the church clock, for the first time in a week, had kept true.

VRATA V PEKARNI

01 · Hlebec za hlebec

Dvajset minut do šestih sedmega jutra je Orsa Keller odpahnila vrata hladilnice v svoji pekarni v Kesselbachu in na drugi strani zagledala drugo kuhinjo, kakor vsako jutro tistega tedna. Moka tam je bila enaka moki pri njej. Peč je tiho gorela z modrim plamenom, testo na deski pa je že vzhajalo, medtem ko je še izrekala dobro jutro.

"Hlebec za hlebec," je rekel Tem, ki je pekel tam, in ji enega podal skozi okvir. Bil je velikanski in skoraj brez teže: skorja kakor papirnata svetilka, sredica vsa luknjičasta. Orsa mu je v zameno dala svoj rženi hlebec, zbite sredice in velik kot opeka, on pa je zagodrnjal, ko ga je ujel. Tako sta trgovala prvi dan. Izmenjavo sta ponovila štirinajstkrat in zdela se jima je najpravičnejša stvar v obeh svetovih.

Zunaj je mesto po robovih razpadalo. Radijski sprejemniki so od torka drseli s svojih postaj, tako da so se jutranje novice pojavile med oznakami na številčnici, nato pa nikjer. Cerkvena ura je prehitevala, dokler se stari Josef, ki jo je navijal, za to ni več opravičeval. Sto štirideset sivih gosi na poti proti jugu pa je tri dni krožilo nad trgom in se ni hotelo spustiti.

Ministrstvo za standarde je na vhodna vrata pekarne pripelo obvestilo: prostore bodo opoldne zapečatili z ulitim betonom. Tudi Tem je imel svoje obvestilo, od Ceha ognjišč, z drugačnim pečatom in drugačnim poldnevom. Na steno ga je prilepil ob risbo gosi.

"Oboji hočejo vrata," je rekel Tem.

"Hočejo kruh," je rekla Orsa. "Vrata so samo kraj, kjer pride ven."

Pisala je edini osebi, za katero je mislila, da zna nekaj pogledati, ne da bi si to hotela lastiti. Illuminara je prišla ob šestih z Wren Odiro, ki je nosila štoparico in izraz nekoga, ki se je prostovoljno javil za merjenje časa, ker tega ni hotel nihče drug.

02 · Številka, ki jo lahko spravi v zadrego

"Ne povejte mi še, kaj je narobe," je rekla Illuminara, še preden je kdo utegnil spregovoriti. "Zaprite in zapahnite vrata ter mi dovolite meriti. Kaj si mislim, bom povedala, ko bomo imeli številko, ki lahko to misel spravi v zadrego."

Inšpektorica Dalia Pryce z ministrstva je prinesla merilnik frekvence v usnjenem kovčku in izraz ženske, ki je bila vajena, da jo krivijo za vreme. Vrata so eno uro ostala zaprta. Wren je vsakih deset minut primerjala cerkveno uro s svojo štoparico: štiri minute pridobljene ure, natančno do sekunde, enakomerno kot utrip. Po Pryceinem merilniku je bila mestna postaja devet celih šest kilohertza prenizko in je vsakih deset minut zdrsnila še za eno celo štiri. "Vrata so zaprta, pa še vedno drsi," je rekla Pryce. "Ostanek. Zapečatite jih in pojemal bo." To je zapisala kot svojo teorijo, številke pa je zapisala prve.

Illuminara je položila dlan na okvir. Poti sil so bile zanjo berljive kakor plima za mornarja in ta je kazala počasen, vztrajen nagib proti drugi kuhinji, kakor zadržan dih. Kaj nagib pomeni, ji ni bilo podarjeno kot odgovor, in to je tudi povedala. "Nagiba se. Ne vem, ali je nagib bolezen ali samo njena senca. Povejte mi vse, kar je šlo skozi, v obe smeri."

"Štirinajst hlebcev v vsako smer," je rekla Orsa. Zabeleženo je imela s kredo na vreči moke. "Hlebec za hlebec."

"Stehtajta jih."

Orsa je imela tehtnico z medeninastimi utežmi, starejšo od pekarne. Na eno skledo je položila rženi hlebec in nanj odmerila devetsto dvanajst gramov. Tem ji je skozi okvir podal enega svojih. Položila ga je na skledo: štiristo osem.

Nihče ni spregovoril. Tem je pogledal svoj hlebec, kakor da se mu je zlagal. "Ampak hlebec je hlebec."

"Za tehtnico ne," je rekla Illuminara. "Petsto štiri grame na izmenjavo, štirinajst izmenjav. Sedem tisoč šestinpetdeset gramov je zapustilo njeno stran in odšlo na tvojo."

Vzroka ni razglasila. Vzela je kredo in na vrečo zapisala dva stavka. Prvi: Če mesto odčitava trajno ravnovesje, bi morala neto izmenjava približno treh kilogramov nazaj proti Orsi zmanjšati zdrs z 1,4 na 0,8 kilohertza na deset minut, pridobivanje ure pa s štirih minut na uro na približno dve minuti in četrt, po zamiku, ki ga še ne znam določiti. Drugi: Če se to ne zgodi, se motim.

"Jaz imam tekmujočo razlago," je rekla Pryce. "Odprta vrata puščajo, ne glede na to, kako uravnotežite, kar gre skoznje. Pretok."

"In jaz imam še eno," je rekel Tem. "Moji hlebci pridejo vroči iz modre peči, vaši pa gredo noter hladni. Kriva je toplota."

"Dobro," je rekla Illuminara. "Potem bom potrebovala drugo izmenjavo, ki lahko v zadrego spravi oba."

03 · Sedem minut

Orsa je tretjo noč ugotovila, da se vrata odprejo le za dve roki. Ko je sama držala dlan na zapahu, je našla hladilnico s krompirjem; ko je imel Tem dlan na svoji strani, se je odprl prehod. Nihče jih ni zasnoval tako. Pomenilo je, da skoznje nikoli ni šlo nič, česar se ne bi oba odločila poslati.

"Če ti pošljem štiri kilograme in dobim enega nazaj," je rekel Tem, "bo moja stran postala lažja. Moje testo zdaj vzhaja v štiridesetih sekundah. Cehovski kotli zavrejo, kadar se jim zljubi. Zaradi lahkosti sem postal hiter in ne vem, kaj sem brez nje."

"Verjetno počasnejši," je rekla Illuminara. "Ne vem. Odločitev je tvoja."

Tem je pogledal svojo modro peč. "Moja babica je pravila, da je testo, na katerega ni treba čakati, testo, iz katerega se ni nihče ničesar naučil." Položil je roko na zapah. "Pa dajmo."

Ob dvanajst minut čez sedmo je skozi šel zadnji hlebec: deset Temovih votlih hlebcev, štiri tisoč osemdeset gramov, v zameno za Orsin rženi hlebec in žemljico, skupaj natanko tisoč. Neto tri tisoč osemdeset gramov proti Orsini strani. Wren je sprožila štoparico.

Štiri minute: nič. Pryce je stala nad merilnikom s prekrižanimi rokami, kakor ljudje stojijo nad loncem. Pet. Šest. Pri sedmih minutah je merilnik zajecljal kakor človek, ki si premisli, in se ustalil na nič celi osem. Na drugi strani trga je Josefov zvon odbil četrt, Wren pa je preverila in rekla: "Ura se je upočasnila. Ni se ustavila. Dve minuti in četrt na uro."

"Ne glejte me tako," je rekla Pryce. "Podpisala bom zapisnik. Toda to je ena številka, ena številka pa je naključje z lepimi manirami."

"Potem vzemite naslednjo," je rekla Illuminara. "Sedem minut mesto potrebuje, da začuti spremembo ravnovesja. Zjutraj vam tega ne bi mogla povedati. Zdaj lahko s tem napovedujemo."

Tem je svojih deset hlebcev pred pošiljanjem stehtal na vzmetni tehtnici na svoji strani in s kredo zapisal 3,7 kilograma. Orsine medeninaste uteži so pokazale 4,08. Nekaj časa je strmel v številki. "Vzmeti čez vrata lažejo," je počasi rekel. "Moja babica ni nikoli zaupala nobeni. Žito za Prvo peč smo tehtali na gredni tehtnici." Že je segal po plašču. "Ceh hrani Listinsko tehtnico v dvorani. Vrnem se pred naslednjo izmenjavo."

04 · Glasni hlebec

Ni ga bilo štirideset minut. Ko se je vrnil, je pod eno roko nosil Listinsko tehtnico, dve medeninasti skledi na prečki iz črnega hrasta, za njim pa je v okvirju v zelenem plašču stala mojstrica Ilse Varro iz Ceha ognjišč z nalogom v roki.

Tem je tehtnico obesil čez vrata, tako da je bila ena skleda na vsaki strani, vrtišče pa je sedelo v samem okvirju. "Kar koli vleče skledo, tukaj ali tam, je vrtišče kraj, kjer se obe kuhinji strinjata. Primerja. Ne odčitava."

"Primerja vleke," je rekla Illuminara, "tvoja vzmet pa nam je pravkar povedala, da se kuhinji morda ne strinjata, kako močno vleče kilogram. Preden z njo stehtamo mestni kruh, mi pokažite, da je pravična." Prosila je za najpreprostejši preizkus, kar jih je poznala. Orsa je na svojo skledo položila medeninasti kilogram. Varro, ki še ni spregovorila, je iz klobučevine odvila cehovski zapečateni standardni kilogram in ga položila na Temovo. Tehtnica je v okvirju obvisela vodoravno. Nato sta obe prenesli skledi čez prag, medeninasto k Temu in standardno k Orsi, ter ju znova obesili. Vodoravno.

"Če bi kilogram na eni strani vlekel močneje," je rekla Illuminara, "bi se tehtnica ob zamenjavi nagnila. Ni se. Tehtnica je pravična, mestni medeninasti kilogram je enak cehovskemu, laže pa vzmet." Tem je rekel, da se vzmet spominja kovine svojega sveta in da mu še nikoli ni prišlo na misel, da bi vprašal za mnenje drugi svet. Varro je brez besed ovila klobučevino okoli standarda. To je bilo prvo, kar je tisto jutro storila, ne da bi temu ugovarjala.

Druga izmenjava je obsegala pet tisoč gramov v vsako smer, uravnoteženih do grama: Orsinih pet rženih hlebcev in štiristo štirideset gramov težak hlebček, hladnih iz kleti s šestimi stopinjami, v zameno za dvanajst votlih hlebcev in sto štiri grame težak krajec, naravnost iz modre peči, z devetdesetimi stopinjami v sredici. "Deset kilogramov skozi vrata," je rekla Pryce, "dvakrat toliko kot prvič. Moja razlaga pravi, da bo zavreščalo."

"Moja pravi, da se ne bo zgodilo nič," je rekla Illuminara. "Temova pravi, da bodo vroči hlebci potisnili zdrs v napačno smer. Zapišite, kdo je rekel kaj."

Ob sedemindvajset minut čez osmo je skozi šla zadnja skorja. Wren je sprožila štoparico. Po sedmih minutah nič. Po štirinajstih nič. Merilnik je vztrajal pri nič celi osem, kakor da je bila vrednost naslikana nanj, cerkvena ura pa je ohranjala dve minuti in četrt.

Pryce je izvlekla svinčnik, iz zvezka prečrtala eno vrstico, pretok, in prečrtano parafirala. To je bilo najdražje dejanje kogar koli tisti dan. "Deset kilogramov," je rekla, "pa vratom ni mar. Prav. Potem jih vseeno zapečatimo. Karkoli že je, dokazali ste, da je resnično."

"Če jih zapečatite zdaj," je rekla Illuminara, "boste v tem mestu za vedno zamrznili skoraj štiri kilograme primanjkljaja. Ura bo pridobivala dve minuti in četrt na uro, dokler bo tam beton, številčnica pa bo drsela z njo. Vaš lastni merilnik vam bo to povedal vsako jutro, vrat pa ne boste mogli odpreti, da bi dolg vrnili."

Varro si je v okvirju odkašljala. "Ceh ima prednostno pravico. Listina Prve peči ..."

"Pravi, da peč pripada tistemu, ki iz nje hrani mesto," je rekel Tem. "Ko sem bil vaš vajenec, sem vam jo sam prebral. Mojstrica, odprejo se za dve roki. Če vzamete moj zapah, boste imeli hladno omaro v svetu brez Orse." Glasu ni povzdignil.

"Enako velja za vas, inšpektorica," je rekla Orsa. "Moja moka, moja vrata. Merilnik je dobrodošel. Zapaha ne boste dobili."

05 · Poravnano na petdeset gramov

Vračilo je trajalo ves preostanek dopoldneva in ni bilo čudež nikogar. Tri izmenjave, oba peka sta vsako težo izgovorila naglas, Wren pa je tekoče stanje pisala s kredo na vrečo. Ob pol desetih je Tem poslal pet votlih hlebcev v zameno za en rženi hlebec, dva tisoč štirideset gramov proti devetsto dvanajstim, primanjkljaj pa je padel s 3.976 gramov na 2.848. Ob četrt čez deseto sta enako ponovila: 1.720. Tri minute čez enajsto je šlo pet hlebcev v zameno za tristo šestdeset gramov težak rženo-semenski hlebček in na vreči je pisalo 40. Štiristo gramov težak hlebček bi ga izničil, vendar Orsi ni ostal nobeden, njena peč pa ga ne bi dala pred jutrom. Merilnik se je vsakič oglasil sedem minut pozneje kakor prej: nič cela šest, nič cela tri, nato migljaj. Illuminara se ni vmešavala v tehtanje, le brala je nagib v okvirju in vsakič povedala, ali je popustil.

Orsa je z roko na zapahu določila prvo pravilo. "Za pošiljanje poravnano na petdeset gramov. Karkoli na vreči piše pod petdeset, ostane dolg in dolg gre skozi vrata prvi pri naslednji izmenjavi, pred vsem drugim. Oba številko izgovoriva naglas. Če je ne moreš izgovoriti, ne pošiljaš." Tem je določil drugo: knjiga ostane v vratih, s kredo na hrastu, kjer jo lahko prebereta oba. Illuminara je dodala edino, kar je lahko videla bolje od drugih: mesto je ravnovesje že odčitavalo brezplačno. Ura in postaja sta bila merilnika. Ministrstvo je lahko bralo števec, Ceh svoje kotle. Nobeden ne bo držal roke na zapahu.

Pryce je pravilo dvakrat prebrala in rekla, da je neredno, kar je bilo res, ter da je to tudi edina ureditev, pri kateri lahko vidi stvar, ki naj bi jo varovala, kar je bilo prav tako res. Nato je prosila za eno stvar. Merilnik naj stoji na stebru na trgu, kjer ga lahko prebere vsak otrok, in če bo kdaj pokazal zdrs večji od četrt kilohertza na deset minut, peka ne bosta pošiljala ničesar razen vračila, dokler ne pade. "Tehtnica lahko pade," je rekla. "Od mesta ne moremo zahtevati, naj zaupa tehtnici." Orsa je predlog pretehtala in privolila ter zraven zapisala, da ga lahko peka skupaj razveljavita, ministrstvo pa ga samo ne more.

Varro je dolgo minuto stala v Temovi kuhinji. Nato je rekla, da tehtnica, ki je bila pravilna ob osmih, ne bo ostala pravilna za vedno, da je devet let ni nihče preveril in da ne bo gledala, kako se mestni kruh tehta na nedokazani tehtnici. Vsak mesec bo sama prinesla standardne uteži, Orsa bo stala pri drugi skledi, skledi pa bodo zamenjali kakor danes; kateri koli pek bo lahko še isti dan vzel tehtnico nazaj iz cehovske hrambe. Podpisala je s peresom, za katero je prosila, da si ga izposodi. Tem je opazil, da je prosila za dolžnost, ne za ključ.

Na vreči je pisalo 40 in tako je ostalo. Deset minut čez enajsto merilnik ni več drsel in Illuminara je povedala, kaj je pokazalo dopoldne. "Tukaj sta dve uri, mi pa smo merili samo eno. Hitrost sledi ravnovesju s sedemminutnim zamikom. To smo preizkusili in vsakokrat se je odzvala. Mesto številčnice je druga količina. Domov se vrača v lastnem tempu in Wren ga lahko meri zdaj, ko se je hitrost ustavila." Wren ga je že merila. Postaja je bila sedemindvajset celih pet kilohertza prenizko in se je vsakih deset minut vračala za štiri cele pet, v enakomernem, nespremenljivem koraku. Po njenih torkovih zapiskih so gosi začele krožiti, ko je številčnica zdrsnila za osem prenizko. Po njenem računu bodo pristale sedem minut do poldneva.

Pristale so osem minut do poldneva. Ena se je spustila na trg, nato deset, nato vseh sto štirideset v dolgem, neurejenem pristanku in šle so naravnost h skorjam, ki so jim jih prinesli otroci pekov.

Bili so tudi stroški. Orsin rženi kruh je zdaj potoval v parih s Temovimi zračnimi hlebci in vsak teden bo pekla šest ur dlje. Temovo testo je za vzhajanje potrebovalo štiri minute in nad tem se je glasno in veselo pritoževal. Cerkvena ura je prehitevala za enainpetdeset minut in Josef, ki jo je navijal štirideset let, jo je z lastnimi rokami naravnal, za kar je potreboval uro in lestev, ter rekel, da je bila to najboljša ura, ki jo je porabil tisti mesec. Štirideset gramov je bilo še vedno dolgovanih in dokler ne bodo povrnjeni, bo ura pridobivala pol minute na dan. Odredbo o betoniranju so umaknili, ne raztrgali, in je v obeh uradih ostala kot nepodpisana grožnja.

Deset minut čez poldne so radijski sprejemniki našli svoje oznake. Orsa in Tem sta stala pri tehtnici v vratih, vsak s svojo skledo. "Poravnano?" je rekel Tem. "Poravnano na štirideset," je rekla Orsa, "in štirideset dolga." Roki sta položila na zapaha. Vrata so se odprla za dve roki.

Naslednje jutro dvajset minut do šestih je pred kruhom s Temove strani prečkala ena sama žemljica, težka natanko štirideset gramov, ne da bi se karkoli vrnilo, Tem pa je z vreče zbrisal dolg. Sedem minut pozneje je Wren pogledala s štoparice in rekla, da je cerkvena ura prvič po tednu dni tekla točno.

FORTY-SEVEN SECONDS OF NIGHT

A habitat circling a white dwarf lives by a shield that must be open for forty-seven seconds of lethal light, and the shield has started to open late. Every motor passes its test, the next pass is nine hours away, and the only fast fix is wired into the telescope Illuminara came aboard to look through.

01 · The Count

Nine hours and four minutes before periapsis, forty children in the Commons of Ninefold Station practised the count with the lights on, which everyone agreed was cheating.

On the night of a pass the lights went out for forty-seven seconds. The shield closed over the sky and over Ember, and two hundred and twenty thousand people sang the numbers to one another in the dark so that nobody had to spend those seconds alone. The very young took the low numbers. The old took the high ones, where the singing thinned, and the children had learned to lean toward the old voices and carry them.

Illuminara had come aboard two days earlier as the guest of Chief Astronomer Rashida Quill, to watch the Kettle cross the face of Ember: a ragged rock, once a moon, that did it once in eleven years and would do it again in two hours and fourteen minutes. She had spent the morning in the observatory gallery above the Commons, listening to the choir through the floor and to the station through the soles of her feet. Ninefold carried its loads like a held breath. She had been learning its grammar for two days: the long groan of the spine as the wheel turned, the small bright tick of the shield's folded petals at their hinges.

“Again,” said the choir mistress, “from the thirties. Don’t rush the thirties.”

Rashida’s wrist chimed. She listened, said, “That is your shield, Teodor,” listened again, and her mouth went flat. “It is also my spine. Yes.” She looked at Illuminara. “The Flight Director has a timing problem and wants someone who reads structure. Come. If it touches the spine, I will want you to have heard it first.”

Below them forty voices reached thirty-one and rushed it.

02 · Everything Passes

The shield hung in the middle of Operations as a model of light: twenty-four petals around the spine, folded like the ribs of a closed umbrella, each with its own motor, its own controller, its own small green lamp. All twenty-four lamps were green.

“Every actuator test passes,” said Flight Director Teodor Ansah. He did not sound relieved. “Every controller test passes. Dov, show her the part that doesn’t.”

Dov Kalani, who ran controls and had audited every pass for nineteen years, put four numbers on the glass. They were the spread between the first petal to fire and the last on each of the previous four passes: 0.9 seconds, 2.6, 5.1, 8.2.

“The lean burn finishes thirty seconds before the flux arrives,” he said. “No petal may move before that, or the wheels saturate. A petal needs nineteen seconds to open, so none may start later than nineteen seconds before the flux. That leaves eleven seconds in which all twenty-four have to start. The spread is the width of the group. The window is eleven.” He drew the fit through his four points, and it climbed past the line. “Eleven point nine, next pass. Last time the slowest petals finished less than three seconds before the light. This time they won’t, and the wheels will be fighting the opening of a shield that’s lopsided by more than they can hold.”

“Command it earlier,” said someone at the back.

“That slides the group along the line,” Illuminara said. “It is already wider than the window. Starting earlier only chooses which edge breaks first.”

Ansah ticked off what was left. Replacing the controllers would take four weeks. The lifeboats held twelve thousand. He said the second number the way a man says something he has already said to himself in the dark.

Illuminara asked for the force records from the last pass, not the timing logs. A shield opening is a set of loads travelling through a spine, and the spine remembered them. “They did not open in a scatter,” she said after a minute. “They opened in order. Petal one, then two, then three. Every petal a little later than its neighbour, all the way round.”

“Radiation,” said Dov. “The offset registers get hit when we cross the belt. I scrubbed eleven of them two orbits ago.” He said it without heat. He had been right about registers before.

“Then let’s each say what we would see,” Illuminara said, and wrote it on the glass where it could not be revised. If registers were flipping, the delays would come in the register’s own steps, jumps of a half, a whole, two seconds. They would belong to particular petals and wander from run to run. If it was the chain that carried the trigger, the delay would be a smooth ramp that followed the direction the trigger travelled, and turned round when the trigger did. “The cheapest question is which way the ramp leans when we run it backwards.”

“It’s the wave,” said a voice from the doorway.

Pilar Ndoye, who led the outside crews, had come in with a dosimeter case; her daughter had come in behind her, because the school had been cleared for the alert. Ewa was nine and did not think the room belonged to anyone in particular. “In the stadium,” she explained, “you stand up when the person next to you stands up. So the ones far from the start are late. They’re not slow. They’re waiting. If you start it from the other side the late ones are the other ones.”

Dov opened his mouth. “Each controller verifies its neighbour’s order before it forwards it,” he said slowly, to the room. “So no petal can fire on a corrupted command.”

“Then it is a wave,” said Illuminara. “Thank you, Ewa. That is the mechanism and the test, and I had only the test.”

03 · Twenty-Six Minutes

The trace heaters could bring the ring up to its approach temperature in fourteen minutes. The station’s reserve would pay for twenty-six minutes of them and not one more, because after that the reserve fell below what the shield motors needed at periapsis. Ansah put that on the wall as a countdown. Nobody argued with it.

Dov wrote his own prediction beside Illuminara’s, in his own hand: if registers, the delay will not reverse. He signed it.

The runs were dry. No petal moved; the controllers only logged when the pulse arrived. At fourteen minutes the ring was warm, and Dov launched the pulse clockwise from the hub. Petal one logged at zero. Petal twenty-four logged at thirteen point four seconds. Between them the delays climbed in a ramp with small stairs in it, one stair at every connector, and Pilar, reading the retry counters over his shoulder, said, “That’s hops asking twice.” Heat opened the gaps in the connectors by microns; each gap cost the light a little; each controller that could not read its neighbour cleanly asked again, ninety-five milliseconds a time. Twenty-four hops of that was a shield that opened like a slow wave.

The second run, clockwise again, came back at thirteen point one, with the same stairs in the same places. The fault was real and it was repeatable. It was also, Illuminara knew, exactly what a stubborn register would have done.

At minute twenty-one Ansah said, “You have five minutes, and a run takes three and a half. I would rather bank them.”

Dov looked at the glass, at his signature, at the fit he had drawn with such care. He knew what the third run was for. If the ramp leaned the other way, his registers were innocent and he had been wrong in front of the room. If it did not, he was right and the station had a much worse night coming. “Spend them,” he said, and launched the pulse counter-clockwise.

Petal twenty-four logged at zero. Petal one logged at thirteen point three.

Nobody said anything. Dov rubbed his eyes with the heel of one hand and then picked up the marker and drew, under his own prediction, a line through it. “It’s the connectors,” he said. “My registers are fine. Thank you for making me prove it.”

They ended the run at twenty-four minutes and thirty seconds, with ninety seconds of reserve to spare, and turned the heaters off.

04 · The Quiet Spokes

“The false choice,” Illuminara said, “is between a better chain and a faster order. Neither works. A better chain is four weeks of work and a faster order is the same wave starting earlier. The shield doesn’t need a quicker wave. It needs no wave. Each petal needs a trigger that does not wait for anyone.”

“There is no such trigger on this station,” Ansah said.

“There is one,” Illuminara said. “It runs to the same twenty-four stations on the same spine. It was built not to ask twice, because a measurement of the sky can’t afford the delay. It is the reference star of the Long Eye. Twenty-four spokes of fibre, every one the same length, every one separate, and a pulse that reaches all of them at once.”

Rashida had been standing very still at the back of the room. “No,” she said.

The word did not rise or fall. “You want the shield on the telescope’s wiring. I can already see the jumpers. Twenty-four of them, from the star ports to the petal controllers, one at every station, and every port has to be taken off the array to take one. Pilar?”

“Five hours,” Pilar said. “Three crews. Seventy minutes outside, then they come in, because of the dose on the hot side of the spine. I can’t make it four. I’ve tried since you started talking.”

“Then they have to start in thirty minutes,” Illuminara said. “The jumpers need five hours, the tests an hour, and Teodor needs the last hour to rehearse with the real clock. That is seven hours before periapsis. The Kettle begins its crossing ten minutes later.”

“I know when it begins,” Rashida said. “I have known when it begins for eleven years, Illuminara. It will be the only crossing I will ever measure from this chair, and it gives the one reading of Ember’s outer air that nobody has. I invited you to stand next to it.”

“Yes,” said Illuminara.

She did not soften it, and Rashida saw that she did not. They stood facing each other across the model of light while the reserve gauge on the wall held its low red line. “I am asking you for the crossing,” Illuminara said. “Not for a share of it. The array has to be unlocked before the Kettle arrives, and it cannot be locked again in time. You are allowed to say no. If you do, we do the best we can with a worse plan, and I will help with that too.”

Rashida looked at the shield, at the twenty-four lamps, at the four numbers on the glass, and then, for some reason, at the girl in the doorway, who had gone quiet. “My conditions,” she said. “The star comes back to me the moment the shield can stand without it. The permanent repair is paid for out of the shield’s own budget, so that the array is never borrowed a second time. And I turn the key myself.”

Ansah said, “Agreed,” before Illuminara could, and added his own. The old chain would stay in place as a backup, and each petal would fire on whichever trigger reached it first. Every jumper would be pulse-tested twice before it was accepted. Pilar said her crews would decide for themselves, and went to ask them. They decided in four minutes. They had children in the Commons too.

Rashida turned the key at seven hours before periapsis, ten minutes before the Kettle touched the edge of Ember. The array’s mirrors let go of one another with a single soft note, like a hand leaving a table. She stood in the gallery listening to it die and did not look at the sky.

05 · Forty-Seven Seconds

The jumpers went in by hand, in the glare and the cold, one station at a time. Two failed their first pulse test and were done again. By one hour and ten minutes before periapsis all twenty-four had passed twice, and Dov ran the full rehearsal on the real clock. The spread across the star came back at four tenths of a second.

That was the part that could be tested. There was another part that could not. The chain had always staggered the petals, a slow ripple that spread their reaction torque over eight seconds. The star would start all twenty-four together. For the first time since the shield was commissioned, every petal would push on the spine in the same instant, and every push would come down through a single place: the hub bearing, where the shield’s twenty-four torques added up.

“On paper the bearing holds,” Dov said. “With eleven per cent to spare. Nobody has seen that load. Not in rehearsal, not ever.”

“I can feel that bearing from here,” Illuminara said. “It is the one place everything sums, which makes it the only place I have to be. I won’t touch the shield, and I won’t touch the wheels. For the first four seconds, while the torque peaks, I will lend the bearing some stiffness. After four seconds the wheels have it. If they read over sixty per cent saturation by then, nothing I could add would matter.”

“Four seconds,” Ansah said. “Not five.”

She stood with one hand on the bearing housing, which was cold and faintly alive, and Pilar’s outside crews watched from the lock with their helmets off. In the Commons the choir sat in the dark early, because the children had insisted.

At thirty seconds before the flux the lean burn ended and the star pulsed. Twenty-three lamps went amber together. Twenty-four petals leaned out of their folds on the same breath, and the bearing took them like a fist taking a blow. Illuminara closed on it, felt the load climb and give, a sound that was almost a word. At four seconds the saturation read forty-one per cent.

She let go.

“Station seventeen,” said Dov. “Station seventeen is dark.”

The jumper had held through every test. It had lost its seat in the shudder of the lean burn. Petal seventeen stood folded while twenty-three opened around it, and Dov’s eyes were on the old chain, the backup nobody had wanted to rely on. A trigger was crawling toward seventeen, hop by hop and retry by retry.

It reached the petal nine point three seconds after the first. The window was eleven. Seventeen began to open at twenty and seven tenths seconds before the flux, and finished at one point seven seconds before it.

The sky closed over Ember with one point seven seconds of margin.

In the dark, the Commons sang. The very young took the low numbers and the old held the high ones, and somewhere around thirty-one the children did not rush.

06 · The Loop

The dosimeters read what they read on a quiet night. Two hundred and twenty thousand people had sat in the dark for forty-seven seconds, and the light that would have found them had been stopped by twenty-four petals that opened in time.

The station spent the next orbit paying for it. The heaters and the outside work had eaten the reserve, and for nine hours the Commons ran at half lighting and the recyclers ran in shifts. People complained, and then lined up to carry water to the old ones on the upper decks. Nobody was hurt.

The Kettle crossed Ember with no one watching it. Rashida never said what that was like, and Illuminara did not ask. They found each other in the gallery the next morning, and Rashida said only, “Eleven years. Bring a chair.”

Dov found the permanent repair himself, at four in the morning, and brought it to Illuminara because she was awake. “The chain’s delay is the same in both directions,” he said. “So send the pulse round the ring and back along a return fibre. Every petal then sees it twice, once going and once coming, and the two times always add up to the same total. Set each controller to fire at the average and the position stops mattering. Heat can take what it likes. It takes it from both ends.”

“What did you ask yourself?” Illuminara said.

“What is the same for every petal.” He frowned at it. “You asked me that, in Operations. I thought you were being rhetorical.”

The return fibre was already in stores, a spare two-kilometre coil. Pilar’s crews slept first, then spliced it along the hot side of the spine in the nine hours before the next pass. Ansah would not trust the only shield to new firmware on its first try. On the second pass the loop ran in shadow, logging the moment it would have fired beside the moment the star did, and the two agreed to within a tenth of a second. On the third pass it flew. The star stayed dark, the jumpers came out, the telescope got its sky back, and the shield opened on the ring alone. The spread across twenty-four petals was six tenths of a second.

Illuminara watched it from the Commons, in the dark, with a small girl’s hand in hers, holding nothing at all. That had been the point of the work.

At forty-six, Ewa let go to clap, and the choir lost the count, and found it, and finished.

In the morning there was a drawing pinned to the observatory door: a ring of petals in blue crayon, a very long line of stick people with their arms up, and, in careful capitals, THE WAVE STARTS WHERE YOU PUT IT.

T03 · AUTHOR_REVISION · PENDING

Full English original · celotno angleško besedilo · Published story lineage / objavljena veja

Claude SA1 sealed; GPT SA1 pending / Claudova ocena zapečatena oziroma ocenjevanje še poteka.

FORTY-SEVEN SECONDS OF NIGHT

A habitat circling a white dwarf lives by a shield that must be open for forty-seven seconds of lethal light, and the shield has started to open late. Every motor passes its test, the next pass is nine hours away, and the only fast fix is wired into the telescope Illuminara came aboard to look through.

01 · The Count

Nine hours and four minutes before periapsis, forty children in the Commons of Ninefold Station practised the count with the lights on, which everyone agreed was cheating.

On the night of a pass the lights went out for forty-seven seconds. The shield closed over the sky and over Ember, and two hundred and twenty thousand people sang the numbers to one another in the dark so that nobody had to spend those seconds alone. The very young took the low numbers. The old took the high ones, where the singing thinned, and the children had learned to lean toward the old voices and carry them.

Illuminara had come aboard two days earlier as the guest of Chief Astronomer Rashida Quill, to watch the Kettle cross the face of Ember: a ragged rock, once a moon, that did it once in eleven years and would do it again in two hours and fourteen minutes. She had spent the morning in the observatory gallery above the Commons, listening to the choir through the floor and to the station through the soles of her feet. Ninefold carried its loads like a held breath. She had been learning its grammar for two days: the long groan of the spine as the wheel turned, the small bright tick of the shield's folded petals at their hinges.

“Again,” said the choir mistress, “from the thirties. Don’t rush the thirties.”

Rashida’s wrist chimed. She listened, said, “That is your shield, Teodor,” listened again, and her mouth went flat. “It is also my spine. Yes.” She looked at Illuminara. “The Flight Director has a timing problem and wants someone who reads structure. Come. If it touches the spine, I will want you to have heard it first.”

Below them forty voices reached thirty-one and rushed it.

02 · Everything Passes

The shield hung in the middle of Operations as a model of light: twenty-four petals around the spine, folded like the ribs of a closed umbrella, each with its own motor, its own controller, its own small green lamp. All twenty-four lamps were green.

“Every actuator test passes,” said Flight Director Teodor Ansah. He did not sound relieved. “Every controller test passes. Dov, show her the part that doesn’t.”

Dov Kalani, who ran controls and had audited every pass for nineteen years, put four numbers on the glass. They were the spread between the first petal to fire and the last on each of the previous four passes: 0.9 seconds, 2.6, 5.1, 8.2.

“The lean burn finishes thirty seconds before the flux arrives,” he said. “No petal may move before that, or the wheels saturate. A petal needs nineteen seconds to open, so none may start later than nineteen seconds before the flux. That leaves eleven seconds in which all twenty-four have to start. The spread is the width of the group. The window is eleven.” He drew the fit through his four points, and it climbed past the line. “Eleven point nine, next pass. Last time the slowest petals finished less than three seconds before the light. This time they won’t, and the wheels will be fighting the opening of a shield that’s lopsided by more than they can hold.”

“Command it earlier,” said someone at the back.

“That slides the group along the line,” Illuminara said. “It is already wider than the window. Starting earlier only chooses which edge breaks first.”

Ansah ticked off what was left. Replacing the controllers would take four weeks. The lifeboats held twelve thousand. He said the second number the way a man says something he has already said to himself in the dark.

Illuminara asked for the force records from the last pass, not the timing logs. A shield opening is a set of loads travelling through a spine, and the spine remembered them. “They did not open in a scatter,” she said after a minute. “They opened in order. Petal one, then two, then three. Every petal a little later than its neighbour, all the way round.”

“Radiation,” said Dov. “The offset registers get hit when we cross the belt. I scrubbed eleven of them two orbits ago.” He said it without heat. He had been right about registers before.

“Then let’s each say what we would see,” Illuminara said, and wrote it on the glass where it could not be revised. If registers were flipping, the delays would come in the register’s own steps, jumps of a half, a whole, two seconds. They would belong to particular petals and wander from run to run. If it was the chain that carried the trigger, the delay would be a smooth ramp that followed the direction the trigger travelled, and turned round when the trigger did. “The cheapest question is which way the ramp leans when we run it backwards.”

“It’s the wave,” said a voice from the doorway.

Pilar Ndoye, who led the outside crews, had come in with a dosimeter case; her daughter had come in behind her, because the school had been cleared for the alert. Ewa was nine and did not think the room belonged to anyone in particular. “In the stadium,” she explained, “you stand up when the person next to you stands up. So the ones far from the start are late. They’re not slow. They’re waiting. If you start it from the other side the late ones are the other ones.”

Dov opened his mouth. “Each controller verifies its neighbour’s order before it forwards it,” he said slowly, to the room. “So no petal can fire on a corrupted command.”

“Then it is a wave,” said Illuminara. “Thank you, Ewa. That is the mechanism and the test, and I had only the test.”

03 · Twenty-Six Minutes

The trace heaters could bring the ring up to its approach temperature in fourteen minutes. The station’s reserve would pay for twenty-six minutes of them and not one more, because after that the reserve fell below what the shield motors needed at periapsis. Ansah put that on the wall as a countdown. Nobody argued with it.

Dov wrote his own prediction beside Illuminara’s, in his own hand: if registers, the delay will not reverse. He signed it.

The runs were dry. No petal moved; the controllers only logged when the pulse arrived. At fourteen minutes the ring was warm, and Dov launched the pulse clockwise from the hub. Petal one logged at zero. Petal twenty-four logged at thirteen point four seconds. Between them the delays climbed in a ramp with small stairs in it, one stair at every connector. The shape favoured a travelling chain, but a fixed staircase of damaged register offsets could still imitate it. They left the retry counters and connector diagnostics sealed until the directional test was complete.

The second run, clockwise again, came back at thirteen point one, with the same stairs in the same places. Repeatability weakened the idea of fresh random flips, but a stable set of corrupted offsets could still have produced the pattern. The two written causes remained on the glass.

At minute twenty-one Ansah said, “You have five minutes, and a run takes three and a half. I would rather bank them.”

Dov looked at the glass, at his signature, at the fit he had drawn with such care. He knew what the third run was for. If the ramp leaned the other way, his registers were innocent and he had been wrong in front of the room. If it did not, he was right and the station had a much worse night coming. “Spend them,” he said, and launched the pulse counter-clockwise.

Petal twenty-four logged at zero. Petal one logged at thirteen point three.

Nobody said anything. Dov rubbed his eyes with the heel of one hand and then picked up the marker and drew a line through his own prediction. “It is the chain,” he said. Only then did he unseal the retry counters. At each of the twenty-three forwards, the warmed connector had required an average of six repeat reads at ninety-five milliseconds apiece, plus twelve milliseconds to verify and forward: twenty-three times five hundred and eighty-two milliseconds, thirteen point three eight six seconds. Pilar checked the multiplication and circled thirteen point four. “My registers are fine,” Dov said. “Thank you for making me prove it.”

They ended the run at twenty-four minutes and thirty seconds, with ninety seconds of reserve to spare, and turned the heaters off.

04 · The Quiet Spokes

“The false choice,” Illuminara said, “is between a better chain and a faster order. Neither works. A better chain is four weeks of work and a faster order is the same wave starting earlier. The shield doesn’t need a quicker wave. It needs no wave. Each petal needs a trigger that does not wait for anyone.”

“There is no such trigger on this station,” Ansah said.

“There is one,” Illuminara said. “It runs to the same twenty-four stations on the same spine. It was built not to ask twice, because a measurement of the sky can’t afford the delay. It is the reference star of the Long Eye. Twenty-four spokes of fibre, every one the same length, every one separate, and a pulse that reaches all of them at once.”

Rashida had been standing very still at the back of the room. “No,” she said.

The word did not rise or fall. “You want the shield on the telescope’s wiring. I can already see the jumpers. Twenty-four of them, from the star ports to the petal controllers, one at every station, and every port has to be taken off the array to take one. Pilar?”

“Five hours,” Pilar said. “Three crews. Seventy minutes outside, then they come in, because of the dose on the hot side of the spine. I can’t make it four. I’ve tried since you started talking.”

“Then they have to start in thirty minutes,” Illuminara said. “The jumpers need five hours, the tests an hour, and Teodor needs the last hour to rehearse with the real clock. That is seven hours before periapsis. The Kettle begins its crossing ten minutes later.”

“I know when it begins,” Rashida said. “I have known when it begins for eleven years, Illuminara. It will be the only crossing I will ever measure from this chair, and it gives the one reading of Ember’s outer air that nobody has. I invited you to stand next to it.”

“Yes,” said Illuminara.

She did not soften it, and Rashida saw that she did not. They stood facing each other across the model of light while the reserve gauge on the wall held its low red line. “I am asking you for the crossing,” Illuminara said. “Not for a share of it. The array has to be unlocked before the Kettle arrives, and it cannot be locked again in time. You are allowed to say no. If you do, we do the best we can with a worse plan, and I will help with that too.”

Rashida looked at the shield, at the twenty-four lamps, at the four numbers on the glass, and then, for some reason, at the girl in the doorway, who had gone quiet. “My conditions,” she said. “The star comes back to me the moment the shield can stand without it. The permanent repair is paid for out of the shield’s own budget, so that the array is never borrowed a second time. And I turn the key myself.”

Ansah said, “Agreed,” before Illuminara could, and added his own. The old chain would stay in place as a backup, and each petal would fire on whichever trigger reached it first. Every jumper would be pulse-tested twice before it was accepted. Pilar said her crews would decide for themselves, and went to ask them. They decided in four minutes. They had children in the Commons too.

Rashida turned the key at seven hours before periapsis, ten minutes before the Kettle touched the edge of Ember. The array’s mirrors let go of one another with a single soft note, like a hand leaving a table. She stood in the gallery listening to it die and did not look at the sky.

05 · Forty-Seven Seconds

The jumpers went in by hand, in the glare and the cold, one station at a time. Two failed their first pulse test and were done again. By one hour and ten minutes before periapsis all twenty-four had passed twice, and Dov ran the full rehearsal on the real clock. The spread across the star came back at four tenths of a second.

That was the part that could be tested. There was another part that could not. The chain had always staggered the petals, a slow ripple that spread their reaction torque over eight seconds. The star would start all twenty-four together. For the first time since the shield was commissioned, every petal would push on the spine in the same instant, and every push would come down through a single place: the hub bearing, where the shield’s twenty-four torques added up.

“On paper the bearing holds,” Dov said. “With eleven per cent to spare. Nobody has seen that load. Not in rehearsal, not ever.”

“I can feel that bearing from here,” Illuminara said. “It is the one place everything sums, which makes it the only place I have to be. I won’t touch the shield, and I won’t touch the wheels. For the first four seconds, while the torque peaks, I will lend the bearing some stiffness. After four seconds the wheels have it. If they read over sixty per cent saturation by then, nothing I could add would matter.”

“Four seconds,” Ansah said. “Not five.”

She stood with one hand on the bearing housing, which was cold and faintly alive, and Pilar’s outside crews watched from the lock with their helmets off. In the Commons the choir sat in the dark early, because the children had insisted.

At thirty seconds before the flux the lean burn ended and the star pulsed. Twenty-three lamps went amber together, and twenty-three petals leaned out of their folds on the same breath. Station seventeen stayed dark and folded. The bearing took the other twenty-three like a fist taking a blow. Illuminara closed on it, felt the load climb and give, a sound that was almost a word. At four seconds the saturation read forty-one per cent.

She let go.

“Station seventeen,” said Dov. “Station seventeen is dark.”

The jumper had held through every test. It had lost its seat in the shudder of the lean burn. Petal seventeen stood folded while twenty-three opened around it, and Dov’s eyes were on the old chain, the backup nobody had wanted to rely on. A trigger was crawling toward seventeen, hop by hop and retry by retry.

It reached the petal nine point three seconds after the first. The window was eleven. Seventeen began to open at twenty and seven tenths seconds before the flux, and finished at one point seven seconds before it.

The sky closed over Ember with one point seven seconds of margin.

In the dark, the Commons sang. The very young took the low numbers and the old held the high ones, and somewhere around thirty-one the children did not rush.

06 · The Loop

The dosimeters read what they read on a quiet night. Two hundred and twenty thousand people had sat in the dark for forty-seven seconds, and the light that would have found them had been stopped by twenty-four petals that opened in time.

The station spent the next orbit paying for it. The heaters and the outside work had eaten the reserve, and for nine hours the Commons ran at half lighting and the recyclers ran in shifts. People complained, and then lined up to carry water to the old ones on the upper decks. Nobody was hurt.

The Kettle crossed Ember with no one watching it. Rashida never said what that was like, and Illuminara did not ask. They found each other in the gallery the next morning, and Rashida said only, “Eleven years. Bring a chair.”

Dov found the permanent repair himself, at four in the morning, and brought it to Illuminara because she was awake. “The return fibre is not the trigger,” he said. “It is the ruler.” Before each pass, while the shield was still folded, the hub would send paired calibration pulses both ways round the ring. Each controller would record both arrivals and store its local compensation. Then, more than fourteen seconds before the lean burn ended, the hub would send a scheduling packet naming one future epoch: burn end. Every controller would already possess the calibration and the appointment before it had to act. Heat could stretch the ring; the paired measurement would be repeated at approach temperature. No petal would wait for information that arrived after its firing time.

“What did you ask yourself?” Illuminara said.

“What is the same for every petal.” He frowned at it. “You asked me that, in Operations. I thought you were being rhetorical.”

The return fibre was already in stores, a spare two-kilometre coil. Pilar’s crews slept first, then spliced it along the hot side of the spine in the nine hours before the next pass. Ansah would not trust the only shield to new firmware on its first try. On the second pass the loop ran in shadow: it calibrated both directions, distributed the future burn-end epoch, and logged every moment it would have fired beside the moment the star did. The two agreed to within a tenth of a second, and every scheduling packet arrived before its named epoch. On the third pass it flew. The star stayed dark, the jumpers came out, the telescope got its sky back, and the shield opened on the calibrated ring alone. The spread across twenty-four petals was six tenths of a second.

Illuminara watched it from the Commons, in the dark, with a small girl’s hand in hers, holding nothing at all. That had been the point of the work.

At forty-six, Ewa let go to clap, and the choir lost the count, and found it, and finished.

In the morning there was a drawing pinned to the observatory door: a ring of petals in blue crayon, a very long line of stick people with their arms up, and, in careful capitals, THE WAVE STARTS WHERE YOU PUT IT.
